While the model merging technique itself is not fundamentally novel, its application to this specific problem, combined with the new resources created and state of the art results achieved, justifies a strong rating. The weaknesses, like limited merging scope and GPT-4o judge dependency, are valid points but do not detract significantly from the overall contribution. The strong experimental results (approaching proprietary model quality in open source setting) and contribution of useful public datasets elevates this paper above a simple application of an existing merging methodology.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Stable Diffusion Models are Secretly Good at Visual In-Context Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.09949v1)
- **Authors**: Trevine Oorloff, Vishwanath Sindagi, Wele Gedara Chaminda Bandara, Ali Shafahi, Amin Ghiasi, Charan Prakash, Reza Ardekani
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLM) in natural language processing (NLP) have demonstrated great potential for in-context learning (ICL) -- the ability to leverage a few sets of example prompts to adapt to various tasks without having to explicitly update the model weights. ICL has recently been explored for computer vision tasks with promising early outcomes. These approaches involve specialized training and/or additional data that complicate the process and limit its generalizability. In this work, we show that off-the-shelf Stable Diffusion models can be repurposed for visual in-context learning (V-ICL). Specifically, we formulate an in-place attention re-computation within the self-attention layers of the Stable Diffusion architecture that explicitly incorporates context between the query and example prompts. Without any additional fine-tuning, we show that this repurposed Stable Diffusion model is able to adapt to six different tasks: foreground segmentation, single object detection, semantic segmentation, keypoint detection, edge detection, and colorization. For example, the proposed approach improves the mean intersection over union (mIoU) for the foreground segmentation task on Pascal-5i dataset by 8.9% and 3.2% over recent methods such as Visual Prompting and IMProv, respectively. Additionally, we show that the proposed method is able to effectively leverage multiple prompts through ensembling to infer the task better and further improve the performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach called SD-VICL (Stable Diffusion based Visual In-Context Learning) that repurposes off-the-shelf Stable Diffusion models for visual in-context learning without any additional training or data. It achieves this by formulating an in-place attention re-computation within the self-attention layers of the Stable Diffusion architecture, explicitly incorporating context between the query and example prompts. The method demonstrates strong performance across six different tasks: foreground segmentation, single object detection, semantic segmentation, keypoint detection, edge detection, and colorization, surpassing existing uncurated-data-based V-ICL methods. The paper also introduces an implicitly-weighted prompt ensembling technique to further improve performance by leveraging multiple prompts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The primary strength of this paper lies in its training-free approach to V-ICL using Stable Diffusion. Existing V-ICL methods often involve specialized training procedures on either curated or uncurated datasets, which limits their generalizability and contradicts the core ICL principle of adapting to new tasks without weight updates. By demonstrating that an off-the-shelf Stable Diffusion model can be repurposed for V-ICL, the paper offers a simpler and more versatile alternative. The in-place attention re-computation and implicit prompt weighting are also technically sound novel elements that contributes to the success of V-ICL.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a critical gap in the V-ICL field by moving away from training-dependent approaches. Its ability to generalize across a diverse set of tasks without fine-tuning has the potential to significantly impact the way visual foundation models are utilized for downstream applications. The results also shows that Stable Diffusion, initially designed for generation, possess significant, yet untapped power for solving vision-related tasks. This has far-reaching implications in utilizing the existing models as the basic building block for more complicated vision-related tasks, thus avoiding the need for building and training specific models from scratch.

**Weaknesses:** A key limitation of the paper is the relatively high inference time associated with diffusion-based models. The paper acknowledges this and suggests exploring faster diffusion techniques. While the method outperforms baselines on uncurated datasets, its comparative performance against methods trained on task-related datasets is not always overwhelmingly superior (as seen in the supplementary material) although consistently overperforms them. The unsupervised prompt retrieval, while effective, could also be explored with other retrieval methods that explicitly take the task at hand in consideration. The paper did not provide any insight in how to best determine the prompts for V-ICL. While the paper performs extensive ablation studies, including the effect of the number of denoising steps, a direct comparison for denoising speed with other ICL methods will further strengthen the paper's claims. Lastly, limited to a few ablation studies, there is a potential lack of discussion around the potential limitations of the model, such as its ability to handle more challenging tasks where it has no prior knowledge, or catastrophic forgetting during a high number of fine-tuning iterations.

**Score Justification:**

Considering both the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7.5**.  The paper's contribution of a truly training-free V-ICL approach is significant and addresses a critical need in the field. The technical soundness of the proposed method and its impressive performance across multiple tasks further solidify its value. However, the computational cost (inference time) and the limited comparative performance over fully-trained models somewhat tempers its impact, thus warrants a less than exceptional rating. Nevertheless, this paper provides a strong foundation for future research in training-free V-ICL.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Performance of GPT-5 Frontier Models in Ophthalmology Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.09956v2)
- **Authors**: Fares Antaki, David Mikhail, Daniel Milad, Danny A Mammo, Sumit Sharma, Sunil K Srivastava, Bing Yu Chen, Samir Touma, Mertcan Sevgi, Jonathan El-Khoury, Pearse A Keane, Qingyu Chen, Yih Chung Tham, Renaud Duval
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) such as GPT-5 integrate advanced reasoning capabilities that may improve performance on complex medical question-answering tasks. For this latest generation of reasoning models, the configurations that maximize both accuracy and cost-efficiency have yet to be established. We evaluated 12 configurations of OpenAI's GPT-5 series (three model tiers across four reasoning effort settings) alongside o1-high, o3-high, and GPT-4o, using 260 closed-access multiple-choice questions from the American Academy of Ophthalmology Basic Clinical Science Course (BCSC) dataset. The primary outcome was multiple-choice accuracy; secondary outcomes included head-to-head ranking via a Bradley-Terry model, rationale quality assessment using a reference-anchored, pairwise LLM-as-a-judge framework, and analysis of accuracy-cost trade-offs using token-based cost estimates. GPT-5-high achieved the highest accuracy (0.965; 95% CI, 0.942-0.985), outperforming all GPT-5-nano variants (P < .001), o1-high (P = .04), and GPT-4o (P < .001), but not o3-high (0.958; 95% CI, 0.931-0.981). GPT-5-high ranked first in both accuracy (1.66x stronger than o3-high) and rationale quality (1.11x stronger than o3-high). Cost-accuracy analysis identified several GPT-5 configurations on the Pareto frontier, with GPT-5-mini-low offering the most favorable low-cost, high-performance balance. These results benchmark GPT-5 on a high-quality ophthalmology dataset, demonstrate the influence of reasoning effort on accuracy, and introduce an autograder framework for scalable evaluation of LLM-generated answers against reference standards in ophthalmology.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper evaluates the performance of OpenAI's GPT-5 series of large language models (LLMs) on ophthalmology-specific question answering using a closed-access dataset of 260 multiple-choice questions from the American Academy of Ophthalmology's Basic and Clinical Science Course (BCSC). The study benchmarks GPT-5 against previous-generation reasoning LLMs (OpenAI's 01, 03, and GPT-40) across different reasoning effort settings.  The primary outcome is response accuracy, with secondary outcomes including head-to-head comparisons using a Bradley-Terry model and evaluation of the quality of generated rationales using an LLM-as-a-judge autograder framework.  The paper also performs a cost-accuracy analysis to identify Pareto-efficient configurations. The key finding is that GPT-5 with high reasoning effort achieved near-perfect accuracy and top-tier rationale quality, but lower-cost GPT-5 configurations (GPT-5-mini) offer strong performance for more budget-conscious applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Relevant and Timely Research Question:**  The paper addresses a relevant question in the rapidly evolving field of LLMs in medicine: how do the newest generation models (GPT-5) perform on specialized medical tasks compared to their predecessors?  Specifically focusing on ophthalmology provides a valuable specialized assessment.
*   **Rigorous Methodology:**  The study employs a well-defined methodology with clear metrics and statistical analyses. The use of a closed-access dataset (BCSC) ensures consistency with prior research and minimizes the risk of training data contamination.  The Bradley-Terry model provides a more nuanced comparison than simple accuracy measures. The inclusion of a cost-accuracy analysis is a valuable addition for practical considerations.
*   **Innovative Evaluation Framework:** The use of an LLM-as-a-judge autograder for evaluating the quality of generated rationales is a novel approach in this context. It offers a scalable and objective method for assessing the reasoning abilities of LLMs, potentially reducing the need for extensive human evaluation.
*   **Practical Implications:**  The paper identifies Pareto-efficient configurations, offering practical guidance for selecting LLMs based on accuracy and cost constraints. The finding that lower-cost GPT-5 configurations can achieve strong performance has important implications for the real-world deployment of these models.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Dataset:** While the BCSC dataset is a standardized and closed-access resource, its size (260 questions) could be considered relatively small. This may limit the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Multiple-Choice Format:** The reliance on multiple-choice questions, while convenient for evaluation, doesn't fully capture the complexities of real-world clinical decision-making. Free-response and multimodal tasks could provide a more comprehensive assessment.
*   **Potential Bias in LLM-as-a-Judge:** The LLM-as-a-judge approach, while innovative, is susceptible to biases depending on the choice of the grading model, the prompt design, and the evaluation format.  This aspect is explicitly acknowledged in the limitations. It should be investigated with a variety of models and prompts to confirm that its judgments are consistent and well-correlated with human judgment (if a human panel is affordable).
*   **Focus on Single Modality:**The study focused only on text based questions. It did not consider any images or other additional data.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies in its early assessment of GPT-5 performance on a medical specialty (ophthalmology), using a closed dataset and a novel LLM-as-a-judge autograding technique. It's significant because it provides a benchmark for future research, offers practical guidance for LLM selection, and introduces a potentially valuable evaluation methodology. However, the significance is somewhat tempered by the limitations of the dataset and the multiple-choice format. The work is an incremental but important advancement.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper makes valuable contributions, it is not a revolutionary breakthrough. It's an incremental step forward in a rapidly evolving field. It offers a thorough and well-executed analysis of a specific LLM generation, but it has limitations regarding data set and validation.
Taking all these aspects into consideration, I assess that the novelty and significance are solid, but not ground-breaking.
