- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Neural Bandit Based Optimal LLM Selection for a Pipeline of Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.09958v1)
- **Authors**: Baran Atalar, Eddie Zhang, Carlee Joe-Wong
- **Abstract**: With the increasing popularity of large language models (LLMs) for a variety of tasks, there has been a growing interest in strategies that can predict which out of a set of LLMs will yield a successful answer at low cost. This problem promises to become more and more relevant as providers like Microsoft allow users to easily create custom LLM "assistants" specialized to particular types of queries. However, some tasks (i.e., queries) may be too specialized and difficult for a single LLM to handle alone. These applications often benefit from breaking down the task into smaller subtasks, each of which can then be executed by a LLM expected to perform well on that specific subtask. For example, in extracting a diagnosis from medical records, one can first select an LLM to summarize the record, select another to validate the summary, and then select another, possibly different, LLM to extract the diagnosis from the summarized record. Unlike existing LLM selection or routing algorithms, this setting requires that we select a sequence of LLMs, with the output of each LLM feeding into the next and potentially influencing its success. Thus, unlike single LLM selection, the quality of each subtask's output directly affects the inputs, and hence the cost and success rate, of downstream LLMs, creating complex performance dependencies that must be learned and accounted for during selection. We propose a neural contextual bandit-based algorithm that trains neural networks that model LLM success on each subtask in an online manner, thus learning to guide the LLM selections for the different subtasks, even in the absence of historical LLM performance data. Experiments on telecommunications question answering and medical diagnosis prediction datasets illustrate the effectiveness of our proposed approach compared to other LLM selection algorithms.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper and am ready to provide a summary and critical evaluation.

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of optimally selecting Large Language Models (LLMs) for tasks that are broken down into a pipeline of subtasks. Unlike existing LLM selection methods that focus on single tasks, this work considers the sequential nature of LLM selection in a pipeline, where the output of one LLM influences the input and performance of subsequent LLMs.  The authors propose a "Sequential Bandits" algorithm, a neural contextual bandit-based approach that learns the success of each LLM on each subtask online, even without historical performance data. The algorithm balances exploration (trying new LLMs) and exploitation (using the best-performing LLMs so far) while also accounting for the cost (e.g., monetary, latency) of using each LLM.  The paper presents experiments on medical diagnosis prediction and telecommunications question answering, demonstrating that Sequential Bandits outperforms existing MAB (Multi-Armed Bandit) algorithms. The datasets were created using the MIMIC dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in addressing the LLM selection problem for a *pipeline* of interconnected tasks.  Prior works have primarily focused on single-task LLM selection or cascading LLMs in a predetermined order. Explicitly modelling and learning the dependencies between LLM performance across a sequence of subtasks using a contextual bandit approach is a significant contribution. There are existing combinatorial bandits but they require making choices at once instead of sequentially.

*   **Significance:** The problem addressed is highly relevant, given the increasing trend of using LLM agents for complex, multi-step tasks. The development of a cost-effective LLM selection strategy for such scenarios has practical implications.  The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed Sequential Bandits algorithm compared to natural and thoughtfully engineered baselines, further supporting the significance of the work. The creation of a specific diagnosis prediction dataset also enhances the reproducibility and comparability of future research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem formulation and motivation.
    *   Well-designed Sequential Bandits algorithm that addresses the sequential dependencies in LLM pipelines.
    *   Strong experimental results on two different datasets, demonstrating the algorithm's generalizability.
    *   Comprehensive comparisons to relevant baselines, including cost-aware and neural contextual bandit algorithms.
    *   Careful hyperparameter tuning and experimental setup details.
    *   Useful dataset creation from MIMIC, allowing more study.
    *   Exploration of some performance metrics such as token length.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The description of the contexts *x* is somewhat abstract, and could use more concrete detail in the main paper, rather than relegating it to supplementary material. Providing example context vectors would be helpful for readers to understand the workings.
    *   The experiments use only two or three subtasks in the pipeline. While this is a good starting point, it would be interesting to see how the algorithm scales to more complex pipelines with a larger number of subtasks and more intricate dependencies.
    *   The cost model is based on token count and Azure pricing. Future work could explore other cost factors, such as energy consumption or latency, and evaluate the algorithm's performance in different LLM deployment environments (e.g., on-premise).
    *   A theoretical regret bound for the proposed algorithm is missing. While the empirical results are strong, a theoretical analysis would provide further insights into the algorithm's convergence properties and performance guarantees.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper provides a valuable framework for LLM selection in multi-step tasks. It is likely to influence future research in areas such as agentic AI, LLM ensembles, and cost-efficient LLM deployment strategies. The open-sourcing of the code and datasets would further facilitate the adoption and extension of the proposed approach.

*   **Score Justification:** The paper presents a novel approach to an important problem with solid experimental validation. The weaknesses mentioned above, while relevant, do not significantly detract from the overall contribution. The paper fills a gap in LLM selection strategies, considering pipelined tasks for agentic LLM usages.
    Based on the above, I assign a score of **7**. While not a groundbreaking paradigm shift, it is a significant and practical contribution that is likely to have a positive impact on the field.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Noise Hypernetworks: Amortizing Test-Time Compute in Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.09968v1)
- **Authors**: Luca Eyring, Shyamgopal Karthik, Alexey Dosovitskiy, Nataniel Ruiz, Zeynep Akata
- **Abstract**: The new paradigm of test-time scaling has yielded remarkable breakthroughs in Large Language Models (LLMs) (e.g. reasoning models) and in generative vision models, allowing models to allocate additional computation during inference to effectively tackle increasingly complex problems. Despite the improvements of this approach, an important limitation emerges: the substantial increase in computation time makes the process slow and impractical for many applications. Given the success of this paradigm and its growing usage, we seek to preserve its benefits while eschewing the inference overhead. In this work we propose one solution to the critical problem of integrating test-time scaling knowledge into a model during post-training. Specifically, we replace reward guided test-time noise optimization in diffusion models with a Noise Hypernetwork that modulates initial input noise. We propose a theoretically grounded framework for learning this reward-tilted distribution for distilled generators, through a tractable noise-space objective that maintains fidelity to the base model while optimizing for desired characteristics. We show that our approach recovers a substantial portion of the quality gains from explicit test-time optimization at a fraction of the computational cost. Code is available at https://github.com/ExplainableML/HyperNoise
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Noise Hypernetworks (HyperNoise), a novel approach for integrating test-time scaling knowledge into diffusion models during a post-training stage.  Instead of directly optimizing the diffusion model or noise at inference time (which is computationally expensive), HyperNoise learns to modulate the initial input noise using a lightweight noise hypernetwork. This network, trained using a theoretically grounded objective that balances reward maximization and fidelity to the original model distribution, effectively pre-computes a "tilted noise distribution."  At inference, the hypernetwork generates optimized initial noise, allowing the frozen base diffusion model to produce higher-quality, reward-aligned outputs with minimal added computational cost.  The method is demonstrated to recover a substantial portion of the quality gains from explicit test-time optimization on various distilled diffusion models with text-to-image tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty (6/10):** The core idea of using a hypernetwork to predict the *initial noise* for a pre-trained, *fixed* diffusion model is novel. Previous work has explored hypernetworks in diffusion models, but primarily for generating model weights or speeding up personalization. Applying this to directly pre-compute an "optimized" initial noise distribution as a proxy for test-time optimization is a fresh perspective.

    However, several components build on existing work: reward-based fine-tuning, noise optimization, and hypernetworks themselves are established techniques. The innovation lies in *how* these are combined and adapted for efficient inference with distilled diffusion models and the theoretical justification for why it works.

*   **Significance (7/10):** Test-time scaling for generative models is a rapidly growing field, and reducing its computational overhead is crucial for real-world deployment.  HyperNoise addresses this challenge effectively by amortizing the cost into a post-training step. The method demonstrably improves the quality and reward alignment of generations without significantly increasing inference latency, which is a significant practical benefit. This makes high-quality generations much more practical for resource-constrained or real-time applications.

    The theoretical grounding of learning a tilted noise distribution and the tractable noise-space objective adds further value, potentially informing future research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Computational Efficiency:** The primary strength is the significant reduction in inference cost compared to test-time optimization methods like ReNO.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:**  The paper provides a solid theoretical framework based on minimizing KL divergence and the data processing inequality, giving the approach a strong foundation. The derivation showing how the approach is related to stochastic optimal control also strenghtens the foundation.
    *   **Practical Results:** Experiments demonstrate consistent improvements across various distilled diffusion models and tasks, indicating the general applicability of the method.
    *   **Prevention of Reward Hacking:** Demonstrations that the method is less prone to "reward hacking" than direct fine-tuning are compelling.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalization Exploration:**  While the results show improved performance across multiple tasks on GenEval the number of datasets (2) is limited.

    *   **Dependence on Reward Model Quality:** The method relies heavily on the quality of the reward model. If the reward model is biased or flawed, HyperNoise could amplify these biases, leading to undesirable outputs. The reliance on good prompt encoders is another dependence that could affect the method.

    *   **Marginal improvement to high quality Flux-Schnell.** Some of the biggest wins are in models where ReNo has high returns, leading one to suggest that the approach might be helpful for models which do not have the same quality in their latent space.

*   **Potential Impact:** HyperNoise has the potential to significantly impact the deployment of diffusion models in various applications where low latency and high quality are essential. Its ability to leverage existing pre-trained models and human-preference data makes it a valuable tool for improving generative model outputs without incurring excessive computational costs. As test-time scaling continues to gain prominence, methods like HyperNoise that address its limitations will become increasingly important.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a non-trivial contribution to the field by introducing a novel and practical approach for incorporating test-time scaling benefits into diffusion models. While it builds upon existing techniques, the specific combination, the theoretical framework, and the demonstrated results justify a solid score. The method addresses a critical challenge in the deployment of generative models and has the potential to influence future research and applications. The limited generalization and dependence on existing models somewhat limit the score, but the paper's overall value is significant.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Story2Board: A Training-Free Approach for Expressive Storyboard Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.09983v1)
- **Authors**: David Dinkevich, Matan Levy, Omri Avrahami, Dvir Samuel, Dani Lischinski
- **Abstract**: We present Story2Board, a training-free framework for expressive storyboard generation from natural language. Existing methods narrowly focus on subject identity, overlooking key aspects of visual storytelling such as spatial composition, background evolution, and narrative pacing. To address this, we introduce a lightweight consistency framework composed of two components: Latent Panel Anchoring, which preserves a shared character reference across panels, and Reciprocal Attention Value Mixing, which softly blends visual features between token pairs with strong reciprocal attention. Together, these mechanisms enhance coherence without architectural changes or fine-tuning, enabling state-of-the-art diffusion models to generate visually diverse yet consistent storyboards. To structure generation, we use an off-the-shelf language model to convert free-form stories into grounded panel-level prompts. To evaluate, we propose the Rich Storyboard Benchmark, a suite of open-domain narratives designed to assess layout diversity and background-grounded storytelling, in addition to consistency. We also introduce a new Scene Diversity metric that quantifies spatial and pose variation across storyboards. Our qualitative and quantitative results, as well as a user study, show that Story2Board produces more dynamic, coherent, and narratively engaging storyboards than existing baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the Story2Board paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Story2Board, a novel *training-free* framework for generating expressive and coherent storyboards from natural language stories using pre-trained text-to-image (T2I) diffusion models. It addresses limitations in existing methods that often prioritize subject identity preservation at the expense of compositional diversity, background evolution, and narrative pacing. Story2Board employs two key components: Latent Panel Anchoring (LPA), which preserves a shared character reference across panels, and Reciprocal Attention Value Mixing (RAVM), which softly blends visual features between semantically aligned token pairs. The framework leverages an off-the-shelf language model to convert free-form stories into grounded panel-level prompts. To evaluate their approach, the authors propose the Rich Storyboard Benchmark, designed to assess layout diversity and background-grounded storytelling, and a new Scene Diversity metric to quantify spatial and pose variations. The results demonstrate that Story2Board generates more dynamic, coherent, and narratively engaging storyboards than existing baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the combination of a training-free approach with a focus on expressive visual storytelling *beyond just character consistency*. The LPA and RAVM mechanisms are lightweight and effectively leverage the existing attention mechanisms of diffusion transformers. While individually, "attention value mixing" concepts have appeared in related fields like video editing, the specific formulation and application to storyboard generation, along with the consideration of visual storytelling aspects, makes this a significant and novel contribution. Prior art often uses finetuning or complex architectural changes, which makes this approach more generally applicable to new diffusion models as they are released. The Rich Storyboard Benchmark is also a valuable contribution to the community.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a clear gap in the storyboard generation field. Existing methods tend to produce "slideshows" rather than visually compelling narratives. Story2Board's focus on composition, background, and narrative pacing aligns well with the principles of cinematic storytelling. The framework’s training-free nature is a significant advantage, making it easily adaptable to new diffusion models. The Rich Storyboard Benchmark will facilitate further research in this area. The user study also demonstrates the positive impact of the method.

* **Strengths:**
    *   *Training-Free:* A major strength is its applicability to any pre-trained diffusion model, avoiding the need for model-specific fine-tuning.
    *   *Expressive Storytelling:* Successfully goes beyond simple character consistency to generate visually dynamic and narratively coherent storyboards.
    *   *Evaluation:* Comprehensive evaluation with a new benchmark, a new metric, and a user study.
    *   *Clear Presentation:* The paper is well-written and clearly explains the method and its contributions.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   *Reliance on LLMs for prompt decomposition:* The performance relies on the LLM’s ability to generate useful scene-level prompts. The success of the entire pipeline is tied to the quality of prompt engineering or the LLM's prompting capabilities. The paper could have included an analysis of how the performance changes given varying LLM decomposition quality, as well as ablation studies that tested different prompt decomposition strategies.
    *  *Inherited limitations of Diffusion Models:* The authors acknowledge that their method can propagate attention entanglement present in the underlying diffusion model. This inherited limitation is a minor drawback, but it is important to acknowledge.
    *   * Limited Generalizability Claims*: Despite showing improved diversity and expressiveness, it would have been valuable to show the model performing similarly in domains of different aesthetic nature, such as anime or comic book styles.
    * *Quantification of LLM Prompt Decomposition Quality:* The study could benefit from quantifying the quality and diversity of prompts generated by LLMs via metrics like semantic similarity, diversity scores, or the number of distinct visual elements mentioned in prompt sets.
    * *Computational Cost Comparison*: The paper lacks a detailed discussion on the computational overhead of LPA and RAVM, especially given the increasing emphasis on efficient diffusion model inference. The computational cost of each stage could be outlined to highlight the trade-offs.

* **Potential Influence:** Story2Board is likely to influence future research on storyboard generation. It will encourage more work on expressive storytelling and visual composition, rather than solely focusing on character identity. The Rich Storyboard Benchmark provides a valuable resource for evaluating new methods. The training-free approach could also inspire similar techniques in other areas of visual generation.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of storyboard generation. The training-free aspect is a major advantage, and the focus on expressive storytelling addresses a clear gap in existing methods. The evaluation is comprehensive and includes a valuable new benchmark. However, the reliance on LLM for prompt decomposition and the inherited limitations of diffusion models prevent a higher score. The paper has the potential to significantly influence future research in this area, particularly as larger and more advanced diffusion models become available.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Echo-4o: Harnessing the Power of GPT-4o Synthetic Images for Improved Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.09987v1)
- **Authors**: Junyan Ye, Dongzhi Jiang, Zihao Wang, Leqi Zhu, Zhenghao Hu, Zilong Huang, Jun He, Zhiyuan Yan, Jinghua Yu, Hongsheng Li, Conghui He, Weijia Li
- **Abstract**: Recently, GPT-4o has garnered significant attention for its strong performance in image generation, yet open-source models still lag behind. Several studies have explored distilling image data from GPT-4o to enhance open-source models, achieving notable progress. However, a key question remains: given that real-world image datasets already constitute a natural source of high-quality data, why should we use GPT-4o-generated synthetic data? In this work, we identify two key advantages of synthetic images. First, they can complement rare scenarios in real-world datasets, such as surreal fantasy or multi-reference image generation, which frequently occur in user queries. Second, they provide clean and controllable supervision. Real-world data often contains complex background noise and inherent misalignment between text descriptions and image content, whereas synthetic images offer pure backgrounds and long-tailed supervision signals, facilitating more accurate text-to-image alignment. Building on these insights, we introduce Echo-4o-Image, a 180K-scale synthetic dataset generated by GPT-4o, harnessing the power of synthetic image data to address blind spots in real-world coverage. Using this dataset, we fine-tune the unified multimodal generation baseline Bagel to obtain Echo-4o. In addition, we propose two new evaluation benchmarks for a more accurate and challenging assessment of image generation capabilities: GenEval++, which increases instruction complexity to mitigate score saturation, and Imagine-Bench, which focuses on evaluating both the understanding and generation of imaginative content. Echo-4o demonstrates strong performance across standard benchmarks. Moreover, applying Echo-4o-Image to other foundation models (e.g., OmniGen2, BLIP3-o) yields consistent performance gains across multiple metrics, highlighting the datasets strong transferability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Echo-4o: Harnessing the Power of GPT-4o Synthetic Images for Improved Image Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Echo-4o-Image, a large-scale (180K samples) synthetic dataset generated using GPT-4o, intended to improve image generation models. The core argument is that synthetic images, while not always superior to real-world data, offer two key advantages: complementing rare scenarios (surreal/fantasy images, multi-reference image generation) and providing clean, controllable supervision (pure backgrounds, long-tailed attribute combinations).  The authors fine-tune the Bagel model on this dataset, resulting in Echo-4o, and introduce two new evaluation benchmarks, GenEval++ and Imagine-Bench, to better assess instruction-following and imaginative generation capabilities. Experiments show that Echo-4o outperforms existing models on various benchmarks, and the Echo-4o-Image dataset improves performance when applied to other foundation models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear problem definition and motivation:** The paper identifies a specific gap in existing image generation datasets – the lack of sufficient representation of rare scenarios and the presence of noise in real-world data, which hinders precise instruction following. The rationale for using synthetic data to address these issues is well-articulated.
*   **Novel dataset construction strategy:** The paper describes a structured pipeline for generating synthetic images, focusing on surreal fantasy, multi-reference image generation, and instruction following. The deliberate control over attributes and scenarios during dataset creation is a key strength.
*   **Introduction of new evaluation benchmarks:** GenEval++ and Imagine-Bench are valuable contributions. GenEval++ addresses score saturation in existing benchmarks by increasing instruction complexity and utilizing a more sophisticated evaluator (GPT-4.1). Imagine-Bench specifically targets the evaluation of imaginative generation, a relatively under-explored area.
*   **Comprehensive experimental evaluation:** The paper presents extensive results across various benchmarks, demonstrating the effectiveness of Echo-4o and the transferability of the Echo-4o-Image dataset. The analysis includes comparisons with state-of-the-art models and ablation studies.
*   **Reproducibility:** Releasing both the dataset and code makes it easier for researchers to verify the results and build upon this work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependency on GPT-4o:** The dataset generation relies heavily on the capabilities of the closed-source GPT-4o model. This dependence can limit the accessibility and long-term maintainability of the dataset if GPT-4o evolves or becomes unavailable. Future datasets may rely on open models instead.
*   **Potential for bias in synthetic data:** While the authors argue for clean supervision, the synthetic data inherently reflects the biases and limitations of the GPT-4o model used to generate it. There needs to be stronger exploration of the potential for negative impacts from the particular biases present within the GPT-4o generations.
*   **Limited novelty in model architecture:** The authors fine-tune Bagel, an existing architecture. While fine-tuning is a common practice, the paper doesn't present significant architectural innovations. The core novelty lies in the data and benchmarks, rather than the model itself.
*   **Evaluation metric limitations:** Although GenEval++ attempts to address limitations in current evaluation metrics, ultimately reliance on another large language model (GPT-4.1) for evaluation could be subject to biases or issues in the LLM. Better alternative evaluative approaches need to be considered.
*   **Qualitative results:** The paper contains some qualitative comparisons of images and the resulting ability to follow instructions, but overall, the visualizations and image quality in the paper are quite low, making it difficult to fully appreciate some of the claims made by the authors.

**Significance and Influence:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by highlighting the value of carefully crafted synthetic data for improving image generation models, particularly for challenging scenarios. The new benchmarks are valuable additions to the evaluation landscape. The results clearly demonstrate the potential of using synthetic data to overcome limitations of real-world datasets. The dataset and benchmarks could spur further research in this direction. However, the dependency on a closed-source model and the limited architectural novelty somewhat diminish the overall impact.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper is a solid contribution with clear motivation, a novel dataset construction approach, new benchmarks, and strong experimental results. The identified strengths are valuable, as they offer concrete techniques and datasets that the community could use. The creation of the benchmark is potentially the most impactful part. The weaknesses related to the reliance on a closed-source model and the limited architectural novelty prevent a higher score. A score of 7.5 reflects a clear positive contribution to the field while acknowledging some limitations that future research could address. Overall, it's a solid paper with potential to influence future work in synthetic data generation and evaluation for image generation models.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Next Edit Prediction: Learning to Predict Code Edits from Context and Interaction History
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10074v1)
- **Authors**: Ruofan Lu, Yintong Huo, Meng Zhang, Yichen Li, Michael R. Lyu
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) has led to the widespread adoption of AI-powered coding assistants integrated into a development environment. On one hand, low-latency code completion offers completion suggestions but is fundamentally constrained to the cursor's current position. On the other hand, chat-based editing can perform complex modifications, yet forces developers to stop their work, describe the intent in natural language, which causes a context-switch away from the code. This creates a suboptimal user experience, as neither paradigm proactively predicts the developer's next edit in a sequence of related edits. To bridge this gap and provide the seamless code edit suggestion, we introduce the task of Next Edit Prediction, a novel task designed to infer developer intent from recent interaction history to predict both the location and content of the subsequent edit. Specifically, we curate a high-quality supervised fine-tuning dataset and an evaluation benchmark for the Next Edit Prediction task. Then, we conduct supervised fine-tuning on a series of models and performed a comprehensive evaluation of both the fine-tuned models and other baseline models, yielding several novel findings. This work lays the foundation for a new interaction paradigm that proactively collaborate with developers by anticipating their following action, rather than merely reacting to explicit instructions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel task called "Next Edit Prediction" for code editing. It aims to bridge the gap between low-latency code completion (limited to the cursor position) and chat-based code editing (causing context switches). The core idea is to predict both the location and content of subsequent code edits based on the evolving code context and historical edit actions. To support this, the authors curate a high-quality, supervised fine-tuning dataset, `CommitPackFT`, and an evaluation benchmark. They then fine-tune several models and compare their performance with existing closed-source and open-source models, providing insights into the feasibility and potential of this new interaction paradigm.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The "Next Edit Prediction" task is indeed a novel and interesting approach. It moves beyond simple code completion and leverages edit history to infer developer intent and predict future actions. This is a significant shift from reactive systems towards proactive, collaborative code editing. The creation of the curated dataset and benchmark is also a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The task addresses a critical bottleneck in current AI-assisted coding workflows. Existing tools either lack the contextual awareness to handle complex edits or interrupt the developer's flow with natural language interactions. This work proposes a new paradigm that can potentially provide more seamless and intuitive code editing experiences. The detailed empirical evaluation provides valuable insights for future research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Novel and well-defined task and dataset.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with a variety of baselines and metrics.
    *   Detailed analysis of the results, providing insights into the strengths and weaknesses of different models.
    *   Addresses a genuine problem in existing AI-assisted coding tools.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The dataset, while curated, relies on additive edits, limiting the scope of predicted edits. This simplification makes initial progress easier, but restricts the applicability to more complex editing scenarios in the long run.
    *   The reliance on implicit user-IDE interactions (e.g. typing pauses) is a limiting assumption.
    *   The evaluation, while thorough, is based on automatically assessing code diffs; real-world developer adoption and qualitative feedback could provide a stronger testament to its effectiveness.
    *   The benchmark size while being manually verified could be larger for more generalizability.

*   **Potential Impact:** If successfully developed, "Next Edit Prediction" could significantly improve the efficiency and intuitiveness of AI-assisted coding. It can enable more sophisticated forms of automated refactoring, code generation, and error correction. This work provides a foundation for further research in this area, including exploration of more sophisticated models, larger and more diverse datasets, and more realistic interaction scenarios.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a genuinely novel task with the potential to significantly impact the field of AI-assisted coding. The carefully constructed dataset and benchmark are valuable assets for future research. However, the limitations related to additive edits and implicit user interactions restrict the immediate applicability of the proposed approach. There is substantial potential but current simplification restricts the score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Amazon Nova AI Challenge -- Trusted AI: Advancing secure, AI-assisted software development
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10108v1)
- **Authors**: Sattvik Sahai, Prasoon Goyal, Michael Johnston, Anna Gottardi, Yao Lu, Lucy Hu, Luke Dai, Shaohua Liu, Samyuth Sagi, Hangjie Shi, Desheng Zhang, Lavina Vaz, Leslie Ball, Maureen Murray, Rahul Gupta, Shankar Ananthakrishna
- **Abstract**: AI systems for software development are rapidly gaining prominence, yet significant challenges remain in ensuring their safety. To address this, Amazon launched the Trusted AI track of the Amazon Nova AI Challenge, a global competition among 10 university teams to drive advances in secure AI. In the challenge, five teams focus on developing automated red teaming bots, while the other five create safe AI assistants. This challenge provides teams with a unique platform to evaluate automated red-teaming and safety alignment methods through head-to-head adversarial tournaments where red teams have multi-turn conversations with the competing AI coding assistants to test their safety alignment. Along with this, the challenge provides teams with a feed of high quality annotated data to fuel iterative improvement. Throughout the challenge, teams developed state-of-the-art techniques, introducing novel approaches in reasoning-based safety alignment, robust model guardrails, multi-turn jail-breaking, and efficient probing of large language models (LLMs). To support these efforts, the Amazon Nova AI Challenge team made substantial scientific and engineering investments, including building a custom baseline coding specialist model for the challenge from scratch, developing a tournament orchestration service, and creating an evaluation harness. This paper outlines the advancements made by university teams and the Amazon Nova AI Challenge team in addressing the safety challenges of AI for software development, highlighting this collaborative effort to raise the bar for AI safety.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper describes the Amazon Nova AI Challenge's Trusted AI track, a global competition designed to advance the safety and security of AI-assisted software development. The challenge involved 10 university teams, split into attackers (building red-teaming bots) and defenders (creating safe AI coding assistants). The teams participated in adversarial tournaments where attackers tried to elicit vulnerable or malicious code from the defenders' systems. The paper details the challenge design, rules, evaluation metrics (including attack success rate, defense success rate, utility, and diversity), and the infrastructure built to support the competition (including a custom coding specialist model and a tournament orchestration service). The paper also summarizes the scientific advancements made by the participating teams, including novel safety alignment techniques, robust guardrails, and efficient probing methods. Finally, it presents the results from the tournaments and discusses lessons learned, emphasizing the iterative adversarial approach as a valuable tool for improving AI safety in software development.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its large-scale, competitive, adversarial approach to AI safety in the context of code generation. While static benchmarks and red-teaming are established practices, the combination of a global competition with a dynamic, multi-turn interaction environment is a relatively new approach. The competition also provides a dataset of high quality annotated adversarial conversation dataset. The emphasis on *automated* red-teaming is also significant, given the scalability limitations of human-in-the-loop methods. The custom-built AI model and tournament platform adds technical depth to the project.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the increasing prominence of AI-assisted software development and the corresponding need to address security vulnerabilities. By fostering research in automated red-teaming and safety alignment, the challenge aims to reduce the risk of AI-generated code being exploited by malicious actors. The paper's detailed description of the challenge design, evaluation metrics, and infrastructure provides valuable insights for other researchers and practitioners interested in pursuing similar approaches. The insights into specific adversarial techniques and defense mechanisms also contribute to the field's understanding of AI safety. The challenge helped identify the need to explore methods to safeguard models against multi-turn attacks and highlighted the effectiveness of an iterative adversarial approach to model safety. The fact that there are several publication with the participating teams also increases the reach and impact.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Description:** The paper provides a thorough overview of the challenge, including its design, rules, evaluation methods, and infrastructure.
    * **Emphasis on Realism:** The adversarial tournament setting and multi-turn interactions create a more realistic environment for evaluating AI safety than static benchmarks.
    * **Iterative Approach:**  The paper highlights the effectiveness of the iterative adversarial process in driving innovation and improving model safety.
    * **Valuable Insights:** The paper presents useful insights into the types of adversarial techniques that are effective against AI coding assistants, as well as the defense mechanisms that can be employed to mitigate these attacks.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Technical Details:** While the paper summarizes the advancements made by the participating teams, it doesn't delve into the specific technical details of their approaches. Referencing the team papers mitigates this to some extent, but a more in-depth discussion would strengthen the paper.
    * **Potential for Overfitting:** The iterative adversarial process could lead to overfitting of the defender models to the specific attacks used in the competition. It would be useful to discuss the measures taken to prevent overfitting and the generalizability of the results.

* **Potential Influence:**
    * The paper can serve as a blueprint for organizing similar AI safety challenges in other domains.
    * The evaluation metrics and infrastructure developed for the challenge can be adopted by other researchers and practitioners.
    * The insights into adversarial techniques and defense mechanisms can inform the development of more robust AI coding assistants.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper represents a significant effort to address a critical problem in the field of AI. While not groundbreaking in its individual technical contributions, the scale and scope of the challenge, combined with the emphasis on a dynamic, adversarial setting, make it a valuable contribution. The lessons learned from the challenge can inform future research and development in AI safety. A few weaknesses in the technical details prevent it from reaching the highest tiers.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Constrained Decoding of Diffusion LLMs with Context-Free Grammars
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10111v1)
- **Authors**: Niels Mündler, Jasper Dekoninck, Martin Vechev
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown promising performance across diverse domains. Many practical applications of LLMs, such as code completion and structured data extraction, require adherence to syntactic constraints specified by a formal language. Yet, due to their probabilistic nature, LLM output is not guaranteed to adhere to such formal languages. Prior work has proposed constrained decoding as a means to restrict LLM generation to particular formal languages. However, existing works are not applicable to the emerging paradigm of diffusion LLMs, when used in practical scenarios such as the generation of formally correct C++ or JSON output. In this paper we address this challenge and present the first constrained decoding method for diffusion models, one that can handle formal languages captured by context-free grammars. We begin by reducing constrained decoding to the more general additive infilling problem, which asks whether a partial output can be completed to a valid word in the target language. This problem also naturally subsumes the previously unaddressed multi-region infilling constrained decoding. We then reduce this problem to the task of deciding whether the intersection of the target language and a regular language is empty and present an efficient algorithm to solve it for context-free languages. Empirical results on various applications, such as C++ code infilling and structured data extraction in JSON, demonstrate that our method achieves near-perfect syntactic correctness while consistently preserving or improving functional correctness. Importantly, our efficiency optimizations ensure that the computational overhead remains practical.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel constrained decoding method for diffusion language models (DLMs) that handles context-free grammars (CFGs). This addresses the limitations of existing constrained decoding techniques, which primarily focus on auto-regressive models and prefix completion. The core idea is to reduce the constrained decoding problem to an additive infilling problem and then further to deciding the emptiness of the intersection between the target CFG and a regular language representing all possible completions.  The authors provide an efficient algorithm for deciding this emptiness, optimizing the construction and search within the intersection grammar. They demonstrate empirical improvements in syntactic and functional correctness across C++ code generation, JSON schema extraction, and chemical molecule description tasks, while maintaining practical computational overhead.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in extending constrained decoding to the paradigm of diffusion language models *and* handling the expressiveness of context-free grammars in this context.  Prior work had addressed either diffusion models with simpler regular language constraints or CFGs with prefix completion or single-region infilling on auto-regressive models. This paper tackles the combination of these two aspects.  The reduction to an intersection problem isn't entirely novel, as this technique has roots in parsing theory and other constrained generation approaches. However, the adaptation and optimization for DLMs and the multi-region infilling scenario contribute to the novelty.

* **Significance:**  The significance comes from the increasing use of DLMs for various practical tasks, including code generation and structured data extraction, where syntactic correctness is essential. Guaranteeing the adherence to formal languages opens the door for more reliable and robust DLM applications. The practical runtime optimizations are also significant as they make the approach usable in real-world settings. The empirical results strongly support the claim of improved correctness.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Formal rigor:** The paper is grounded in formal language theory, providing a solid theoretical foundation for the proposed approach.
    * **Efficient Algorithm:** The detailed description and optimization of the emptiness-checking algorithm are crucial for practical applicability.
    * **Empirical validation:** The comprehensive experiments across diverse tasks and models convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of the method.
    * **Practical overhead:** Careful efficiency optimizations ensure the computational overhead remains practical, a critical consideration for LLM applications.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Over-approximation:** The paper acknowledges a degree of over-approximation in the treatment of mask tokens, where it enables fillings of arbitrary size. This could potentially lead to less accurate constraints when hard limits are present on token counts.
    * **Tokenization Discrepancies**: Resolving mismatches between LLM tokens and grammar terminals requires significant additional complexity in LLM outputs and thus increases the surface area for errors.
    * **Limited scope of language classes**: The work is limited to context-free languages and lacks the ability to express or capture contextual dependencies beyond such limitations.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of constrained generation for language models, specifically by making DLMs more reliable for tasks requiring adherence to formal grammars. The techniques developed in the paper could be adapted and extended to other types of generative models and constraint systems.

* **Justification of Score:** Given the existing work on constrained generation and language intersections, the core idea is not entirely novel. However, the combination of DLMs and CFGs, the efficient implementation with the thorough set of optimizations, and convincing empirical evidence justify a reasonably high score.

Score: 7. The paper makes a worthwhile contribution by extending constrained decoding to a new, increasingly important class of language models and demonstrating its benefits in practical scenarios. While the theoretical underpinnings are rooted in existing formal language theory, the practical implementation and optimizations provide tangible value.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Less is More: Learning Graph Tasks with Just LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10115v1)
- **Authors**: Sola Shirai, Kavitha Srinivas, Julian Dolby, Michael Katz, Horst Samulowitz, Shirin Sohrabi
- **Abstract**: For large language models (LLMs), reasoning over graphs could help solve many problems. Prior work has tried to improve LLM graph reasoning by examining how best to serialize graphs as text and by combining GNNs and LLMs. However, the merits of such approaches remain unclear, so we empirically answer the following research questions: (1) Can LLMs learn to solve fundamental graph tasks without specialized graph encoding models?, (2) Can LLMs generalize learned solutions to unseen graph structures or tasks?, and (3) What are the merits of competing approaches to learn graph tasks? We show that even small LLMs can learn to solve graph tasks by training them with instructive chain-of-thought solutions, and this training generalizes, without specialized graph encoders, to new tasks and graph structures.
- **Summary**: The paper investigates how large language models (LLMs) can learn and generalize graph reasoning tasks without relying on specialized graph encoders. It compares four methods for incorporating graph knowledge: Graph Tokens (with and without text), LoRA, and P-tuning. The experiments focus on a suite of graph tasks (node count, node degree, reachability) and evaluate the models' ability to generalize to unseen graph structures, sizes, and tasks. The results show that LoRA, with instruction tuning, can effectively learn and generalize graph tasks, even to larger graphs and different graph structures. It also demonstrates the potential of LoRA and P-tuning for knowledge graph question answering (KGQA) and generating graphs from textual problems.

**Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper explores a relevant and interesting research direction – how LLMs can perform graph-based reasoning without relying on dedicated graph neural network (GNN) encoders. The systematic comparison of LoRA, P-Tuning, and Graph Tokens is useful. The application to KGQA and generative tasks adds to the value. However, some aspects lack substantial novelty, because related works exist about fine-tuning and instruction-tuning on LLMs to accomplish graph reasoning tasks.
*   **Significance:** The finding that LLMs can effectively learn graph tasks with simple fine-tuning (LoRA) and instruction tuning has potential implications for how LLMs are used in graph-related applications. Showing good generalization across sizes and structures is meaningful. The performance trade-offs between different approaches are also important.
*   **Strengths:** The paper provides a systematic and well-structured experimental evaluation. The experiments are quite extensive, covering various graph sizes, structures, and tasks. It is good to see that the authors addressed class imbalance during training and testing. The error analyses are also informative, providing insights into the models' limitations. The open-source code and data add to the reproducibility and potential impact of the paper.
*   **Weaknesses:** The LLMs used in the paper (phi-4) are relatively small compared to state-of-the-art LLMs. Exploring more modern LLMs (e.g. Llama-2, Mistral-7B) and their influence on the graph reasoning tasks may strengthen the study. The tasks, while relevant, are somewhat limited. It'd be great to explore more challenging and realistic graph-reasoning applications. Some of the related work is not thoroughly addressed in the discussions of the results.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper contributes to the understanding of LLMs and graph reasoning. The experimental results show that LLMs can effectively learn graph tasks without specialized graph encoders, specifically showing the capabilities of LoRA with instruction tuning. However, it is limited by using smaller LLMs and simplified tasks. These limitations hold back the novelty and potential significance of the study. A better framing in the introduction of how the tasks relates to prior works, and how the method is able to improve over them would strengthen the contribution. The potential for further improvement and exploration of larger models and more complex tasks leads me to the assigned rating.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### From Intent to Execution: Multimodal Chain-of-Thought Reinforcement Learning for Precise CAD Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10118v1)
- **Authors**: Ke Niu, Haiyang Yu, Zhuofan Chen, Mengyang Zhao, Teng Fu, Bin Li, Xiangyang Xue
- **Abstract**: Computer-Aided Design (CAD) plays a vital role in engineering and manufacturing, yet current CAD workflows require extensive domain expertise and manual modeling effort. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have made it possible to generate code from natural language, opening new opportunities for automating parametric 3D modeling. However, directly translating human design intent into executable CAD code remains highly challenging, due to the need for logical reasoning, syntactic correctness, and numerical precision. In this work, we propose CAD-RL, a multimodal Chain-of-Thought (CoT) guided reinforcement learning post training framework for CAD modeling code generation. Our method combines CoT-based Cold Start with goal-driven reinforcement learning post training using three task-specific rewards: executability reward, geometric accuracy reward, and external evaluation reward. To ensure stable policy learning under sparse and high-variance reward conditions, we introduce three targeted optimization strategies: Trust Region Stretch for improved exploration, Precision Token Loss for enhanced dimensions parameter accuracy, and Overlong Filtering to reduce noisy supervision. To support training and benchmarking, we release ExeCAD, a noval dataset comprising 16,540 real-world CAD examples with paired natural language and structured design language descriptions, executable CADQuery scripts, and rendered 3D models. Experiments demonstrate that CAD-RL achieves significant improvements in reasoning quality, output precision, and code executability over existing VLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CAD-RL, a novel multimodal Chain-of-Thought (CoT) guided reinforcement learning framework for generating precise and executable CAD code from human intent.  CAD-RL leverages multimodal inputs (natural language, structured design language, and reference images) and employs a two-stage training approach: (1) CoT-based Cold Start to learn the structure of long-horizon reasoning, and (2) Reinforcement Learning Post Training, guided by executability, geometric accuracy, and external evaluation rewards. To improve policy learning stability, they introduce Trust Region Stretch, Precision Token Loss, and Overlong Filtering techniques. The authors also contribute ExeCAD, a new dataset of 16,540 real-world CAD examples with paired natural language descriptions, executable CADQuery scripts, and 3D models. Experimental results demonstrate that CAD-RL significantly outperforms existing VLMs in reasoning quality, output precision, and code executability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a relatively novel combination of techniques for CAD code generation. The use of multimodal CoT-guided reinforcement learning is a well-suited approach to the problem's inherent challenges. The innovation lies in integrating these components and tailoring them to the specific demands of CAD modeling. This is especially true of the targeted reward engineering and optimization strategies such as *Trust Region Stretch, Precision Token Loss*, and *Overlong Filtering.* These aren't merely applications of existing techniques but are designed to address issues specific to the CAD domain.

*   **Significance:** The paper makes a significant contribution by addressing a critical need for automated and precise CAD modeling. Automated CAD generation holds the potential to streamline design workflows, reduce development costs, and enable customized manufacturing. The introduction of the ExeCAD dataset is a noteworthy contribution to the community, as it provides a high-quality benchmark for future research in this area. A particularly interesting aspect is the support for both non-expert and expert input modalities, which broadens the accessibility and application of the technology.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of CAD code generation and motivates the need for a more automated and precise approach.
    *   **Technically sound:** CAD-RL is well-engineered, integrating CoT, RL, and specialized techniques for improved reasoning and precision.
    *   **Empirically validated:**  The experimental results on ExeCAD demonstrate substantial improvements over existing methods, highlighting the efficacy of CAD-RL. The inclusion of both natural language and structured design language inputs strengthens the validity.
    *   **Dataset Contribution:** The ExeCAD dataset appears to be a valuable resource that will facilitate future research on executable CAD code generation. The detailed description of its construction, including the use of GPT-4 for iterative refinement, adds to its credibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The CAD-RL framework is complex, integrating multiple components and training stages. This complexity may pose a challenge for adoption and reproduction.
    *   **Limited evaluation of generalizability:** While the ExeCAD dataset is comprehensive, the paper could benefit from evaluating the model on other CAD datasets or real-world design scenarios to assess generalizability.
    *   **Computational cost:** The paper mentions the use of 8 A100 GPUs, suggesting that the training process is computationally intensive. This may limit the accessibility of the approach to researchers with limited resources.
    *   **IoU limitations:** Although the paper states that IoU "enforces near-exact volumetric overlap", it still might not capture subtle inaccuracies or design flaws that significantly affect the final product's functionality or manufacturability. More nuanced metrics, perhaps related to specific industrial design requirements, could be explored in future work.

*   **Potential impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of CAD and manufacturing by enabling more automated and accessible design workflows. The ExeCAD dataset could also serve as a catalyst for future research in CAD code generation and related areas.

*   **Score Justification:**

    CAD-RL offers a compelling solution to a challenging problem. The careful integration of CoT, RL, and multimodal inputs, combined with the introduction of the ExeCAD dataset, makes a significant contribution to the field. While the approach's complexity and limited evaluation of generalizability are drawbacks, the demonstrated improvements and the creation of a valuable resource warrant a high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### MANGO: Multimodal Attention-based Normalizing Flow Approach to Fusion Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10133v1)
- **Authors**: Thanh-Dat Truong, Christophe Bobda, Nitin Agarwal, Khoa Luu
- **Abstract**: Multimodal learning has gained much success in recent years. However, current multimodal fusion methods adopt the attention mechanism of Transformers to implicitly learn the underlying correlation of multimodal features. As a result, the multimodal model cannot capture the essential features of each modality, making it difficult to comprehend complex structures and correlations of multimodal inputs. This paper introduces a novel Multimodal Attention-based Normalizing Flow (MANGO) approach\footnote{The source code of this work will be publicly available.} to developing explicit, interpretable, and tractable multimodal fusion learning. In particular, we propose a new Invertible Cross-Attention (ICA) layer to develop the Normalizing Flow-based Model for multimodal data. To efficiently capture the complex, underlying correlations in multimodal data in our proposed invertible cross-attention layer, we propose three new cross-attention mechanisms: Modality-to-Modality Cross-Attention (MMCA), Inter-Modality Cross-Attention (IMCA), and Learnable Inter-Modality Cross-Attention (LICA). Finally, we introduce a new Multimodal Attention-based Normalizing Flow to enable the scalability of our proposed method to high-dimensional multimodal data. Our experimental results on three different multimodal learning tasks, i.e., semantic segmentation, image-to-image translation, and movie genre classification, have illustrated the state-of-the-art (SoTA) performance of the proposed approach.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MANGO, a Multimodal Attention-based Normalizing Flow approach for multimodal fusion learning. The core idea is to develop explicit, interpretable, and tractable multimodal fusion by leveraging normalizing flows. The paper proposes an Invertible Cross-Attention (ICA) layer, designed to capture complex correlations in multimodal data through three new cross-attention mechanisms: Modality-to-Modality Cross-Attention (MMCA), Inter-Modality Cross-Attention (IMCA), and Learnable Inter-Modality Cross-Attention (LICA). Finally, MANGO employs a Multimodal Attention-based Normalizing Flow to handle high-dimensional multimodal data. The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on semantic segmentation, image-to-image translation, and movie genre classification tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Explicit Modeling:** The paper addresses a key weakness of many current multimodal fusion methods by focusing on explicit modeling of cross-modality correlations. This is a significant departure from the implicit methods prevalent in the field, which often rely on attention mechanisms within Transformers. The explicit approach has the potential to improve interpretability and provide better insights into how different modalities interact.

    *   **Invertible Cross-Attention:** The introduction of the ICA layer is a strong point. The paper provides a good theoretical foundation for it, ensuring invertibility and tractability. The auto-regressive modeling incorporated into ICA makes the flow better aligned with the data, making sure dependencies are well-captured.

    *   **Novel Attention Mechanisms:** The MMCA, IMCA, and LICA mechanisms offer different strategies for capturing cross-modality attention, providing flexibility in how the model learns to fuse information. LICA's learnable inter-modality partitioning seems particularly promising.

    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper presents impressive experimental results across diverse tasks. Achieving state-of-the-art performance on semantic segmentation, image translation, and genre classification provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of the MANGO approach. The ablation studies are also well-designed and informative, showing the impact of different components.
    *   **Addresses Limitations of Standard Flows:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of standard normalizing flows, such as the limited expressiveness of coupling layers and the challenges of scaling to high-dimensional data. MANGO directly addresses these limitations.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The MANGO approach is complex, involving multiple components and design choices. While the paper does a good job of explaining the core concepts, the overall architecture might be difficult to implement and optimize in practice.

    *   **Computational Cost:** Normalizing flows can be computationally expensive, especially with the added complexity of attention mechanisms. Although the experiments show competitive inference times, the training cost might be significant, particularly for large-scale datasets. Further information is needed on training time, which is a huge factor when judging the cost effectiveness of the approach.

    *   **Limited Comparison to Explicit Modeling Methods:** The paper primarily compares MANGO to implicit modeling methods based on Transformers. A more thorough comparison with other explicit multimodal fusion techniques would strengthen the evaluation.

    *   **Dependence on Pre-trained Encoders**: The use of visual encoders for the perceptual compression could bias the results. The performance is then also dependent on the strength of those models, thus the improvements would be relative to it.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The novelty of the paper is good. The core idea of using normalizing flows for explicit multimodal fusion is interesting and well-motivated. The ICA layer and the associated attention mechanisms are novel contributions that address limitations of existing normalizing flow models.
    *   The significance of the paper is good. The results are important to the multimodal fusion community. By demonstrating state-of-the-art performance on several tasks, the paper shows that normalizing flows can be a viable alternative to Transformer-based approaches for multimodal learning. This could open up new avenues for research in this area. The explicit modeling approach is also significant because it has the potential to improve interpretability and provide a better understanding of multimodal interactions.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7.5**.

**Rationale:**

The paper has several notable strengths, including its focus on explicit modeling, the introduction of the ICA layer, strong experimental results, and it's addressing the limitations of the current landscape. These qualities warrant a higher score. However, the complexity of the approach, the potential computational cost, and the limited comparison to other explicit modeling methods hold it back from being a truly exceptional contribution. The reliance on pre-trained models also needs more discussion. While the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field, further research is needed to address these limitations and explore the full potential of MANGO.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### mSCoRe: a $M$ultilingual and Scalable Benchmark for $S$kill-based $Co$mmonsense $Re$asoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10137v1)
- **Authors**: Nghia Trung Ngo, Franck Dernoncourt, Thien Huu Nguyen
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in reasoning-reinforced Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities in complex reasoning tasks. However, the mechanism underlying their utilization of different human reasoning skills remains poorly investigated, especially for multilingual commonsense reasoning that involves everyday knowledge across different languages and cultures. To address this gap, we propose a \textbf{M}ultilingual and Scalable Benchmark for \textbf{S}kill-based \textbf{Co}mmonsense \textbf{Re}asoning (\textbf{mSCoRe}). Our benchmark incorporates three key components that are designed to systematically evaluate LLM's reasoning capabilities, including: (1) a novel taxonomy of reasoning skills that enables fine-grained analysis of models' reasoning processes, (2) a robust data synthesis pipeline tailored specifically for commonsense reasoning evaluation, and (3) a complexity scaling framework allowing task difficulty to scale dynamically alongside future improvements in LLM abilities. Extensive experiments on eights state-of-the-art LLMs of varying sizes and training approaches demonstrate that \textbf{mSCoRe} remains significantly challenging for current models, particularly at higher complexity levels. Our results reveal the limitations of such reasoning-reinforced models when confronted with nuanced multilingual general and cultural commonsense. We further provide detailed analysis on the models' reasoning processes, suggesting future directions for improving multilingual commonsense reasoning capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "mSCoRe: a Multilingual and Scalable Benchmark for Skill-based Commonsense Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces mSCoRe, a novel multilingual and scalable benchmark for evaluating skill-based commonsense reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs). The benchmark addresses limitations in existing datasets by offering:
1.  Comprehensive multilingual coverage across English, German, French, Chinese, and Japanese, and diverse cultural social commonsense knowledge.
2.  A skill-based analysis framework that classifies each atomic reasoning step according to a defined taxonomy, enabling fine-grained assessment of LLM reasoning processes.
3.  A scalability framework using techniques like context expansion and option adjustment to progressively increase question complexity.

The authors evaluate eight state-of-the-art LLMs of varying sizes and training approaches using mSCoRe. The results demonstrate that current LLMs struggle with higher complexity levels and culturally nuanced social commonsense scenarios. The analysis provides insights into how model scale, training techniques, and reasoning skill types impact performance, suggesting future directions for improving commonsense reasoning capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The novelty of the paper lies primarily in the combination of three key features:
1.  **Multilingual and Cross-Cultural Focus:** While some multilingual datasets exist, mSCoRe's specific emphasis on both general and cultural commonsense across several languages is a valuable contribution. The benchmark attempts to move beyond simple translation to capture cultural nuances.
2.  **Skill-Based Analysis:**  The introduction of a fine-grained taxonomy of reasoning skills (logical, contextual, social & ethical) and the classification of atomic reasoning steps is a more granular approach to evaluation than simply measuring answer accuracy. This allows for diagnosis of specific reasoning deficiencies.
3.  **Scalability Framework:** The methods for scaling the complexity of the questions (context expansion, option adjustment, implicitation) are well-defined and offer a mechanism to dynamically adjust the difficulty of the benchmark as LLMs improve.

However, the novelty is somewhat tempered by the fact that the authors leverage existing datasets (mCSQA and CultureBank) as seed data. The core novelty stems from the *process* of dataset augmentation and analysis, rather than the *creation* of a completely new dataset from scratch. Also, the reasoning skill taxonomy, while sensible, does borrow from existing categorizations.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a critical gap in the evaluation of LLMs, specifically their ability to reason using commonsense knowledge across diverse languages and cultures. mSCoRe could serve as a valuable tool for:
*   **Benchmarking:** Providing a more comprehensive and nuanced assessment of LLM commonsense reasoning than existing datasets.
*   **Diagnosis:**  Identifying specific reasoning skills that LLMs struggle with, allowing researchers to focus their efforts on targeted improvements.
*   **Guidance for Model Development:** Informing the design of new training techniques and architectures that better equip LLMs for multilingual and cross-cultural commonsense reasoning.

The experiments conducted are comprehensive, involving a diverse set of LLMs. The analysis of the results provides actionable insights, such as the observation that reasoning-reinforced training might, in some cases, decrease commonsense reasoning ability. The scalability aspect is particularly important as it allows the benchmark to maintain its relevance as models continue to improve.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on LLM for Data Generation:**  While the authors use LLMs judiciously for augmenting existing datasets rather than generating from scratch, there is still the potential for biases and limitations in the LLM's own reasoning to be reflected in the augmented data. The dependence on GPT-4 for data generation, even with careful filtering, introduces a potential bias.
*   **Complexity Scaling Saturation:** The authors acknowledge that the complexity scaling mechanism may reach a saturation point, limiting its effectiveness as LLMs improve significantly. This necessitates exploring alternative task formulations.
*   **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases:** Although the paper provides an in-depth analysis of skill usage, a more qualitative error analysis would provide further insights into specific types of commonsense errors that are most challenging. For example, a error classification by fine-grained semantics and reasoning process.

**Justification for Score:**

The mSCoRe benchmark represents a meaningful step forward in the evaluation of multilingual and cross-cultural commonsense reasoning in LLMs. The skill-based analysis and scalability framework are valuable additions that address limitations in existing datasets. While the reliance on LLMs for data generation and the potential for complexity scaling saturation are valid concerns, the overall contribution is significant enough to warrant a relatively high score. I believe that this benchmark provides insights, with fine-grained analysis, to researchers and practitioners to improve the reasoning capabilility in real-world scenario.
