- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Multi-Turn Puzzles: Evaluating Interactive Reasoning and Strategic Dialogue in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10142v1)
- **Authors**: Kartikeya Badola, Jonathan Simon, Arian Hosseini, Sara Marie Mc Carthy, Tsendsuren Munkhdalai, Abhimanyu Goyal, Tomáš Kočiský, Shyam Upadhyay, Bahare Fatemi, Mehran Kazemi
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) excel at solving problems with clear and complete statements, but often struggle with nuanced environments or interactive tasks which are common in most real-world scenarios. This highlights the critical need for developing LLMs that can effectively engage in logically consistent multi-turn dialogue, seek information and reason with incomplete data. To this end, we introduce a novel benchmark comprising a suite of multi-turn tasks each designed to test specific reasoning, interactive dialogue, and information-seeking abilities. These tasks have deterministic scoring mechanisms, thus eliminating the need for human intervention. Evaluating frontier models on our benchmark reveals significant headroom. Our analysis shows that most errors emerge from poor instruction following, reasoning failures, and poor planning. This benchmark provides valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of current LLMs in handling complex, interactive scenarios and offers a robust platform for future research aimed at improving these critical capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new benchmark called "Multi-Turn Puzzles" (MTP) designed to evaluate the interactive reasoning, strategic dialogue, and information-seeking abilities of large language models (LLMs).  The benchmark consists of five distinct tasks: Word Guess, Movie Recommendation, Circuit Decoding, Word Chaining, and Twenty Questions, each designed to test specific cognitive skills required for multi-turn problem-solving. The tasks are synthetically generated, scored deterministically, and eliminate the need for human evaluation. The authors evaluate several frontier LLMs (Gemini 2.5 Pro Exp, Claude 3.5 Sonnet, and OpenAI GPT-40) on MTP, revealing significant headroom for improvement in most tasks, especially in areas like efficient information seeking and complex deduction. They also analyze the cost-performance trade-offs within the Gemini family, investigate the ability of stronger models to continue conversations started by weaker models, and perform a qualitative error analysis to identify key failure modes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a Critical Gap:**  The paper tackles a real and important limitation of existing LLM benchmarks, which often focus on single-turn tasks and do not adequately assess interactive reasoning and dialogue skills. Many real-world applications necessitate this capability.
    *   **Novel Benchmark Design:** The tasks within MTP are well-designed to isolate specific aspects of multi-turn reasoning. The use of synthetic data with deterministic scoring ensures objective and reliable evaluation. The variety of tasks (deductive reasoning, information seeking, logical deduction, etc.) provides a comprehensive assessment.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough evaluation of multiple state-of-the-art LLMs. The inclusion of cost-performance trade-offs and the inter-model experiments (strong model continuing weak model's conversation) are insightful.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The qualitative error analysis provides valuable insights into the failure modes of LLMs in multi-turn scenarios. This helps guide future research towards improving specific weaknesses.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Synthetic Tasks:** While the synthetic nature enables precise control and objective scoring, it may limit the benchmark's ability to fully capture the complexities of real-world interactions. The tasks are puzzles rather than full-fledged dialogue environments.
    *   **Limited Complexity:** Compared to some other LLM benchmarks, the individual tasks in MTP, while insightful, don't involve extensive or open-ended interactions.
    *   **Generalizability:** The tasks rely on rather simplistic environments (e.g., movie recommendation based on limited pre-defined attributes), which may not fully represent real-world complexities. Generalizing the conclusions to more intricate scenarios may present limitations.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The primary novelty of the paper lies in the *creation of the MTP benchmark itself* rather than in significantly advancing our theoretical understanding of LLMs. By providing a new suite of tasks specifically tailored to assess multi-turn reasoning and interaction, the paper offers a valuable tool for the community to measure progress in this critical area. Its value lies in its ability to *reliably* and *efficiently* assess shortcomings with a view towards better designs.

*   **Justification of Score:**
        Given the above analysis, a reasonable score would be **7**. The paper makes a solid contribution by identifying a gap in existing evaluation methods and creating a novel benchmark to address it. The benchmark design is well-considered, and the initial evaluation provides valuable insights.  However, the relatively low complexity of the tasks and the purely synthetic nature somewhat limit its impact compared to benchmarks grounded in real-world data. Its ultimate impact depends on how widely adopted the benchmark becomes and whether it spurs significant progress in multi-turn interaction research. A score higher than 7 would require a more foundational theoretical advance or validation across more complex real-world environments.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Agentic AI Frameworks: Architectures, Protocols, and Design Challenges
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10146v1)
- **Authors**: Hana Derouiche, Zaki Brahmi, Haithem Mazeni
- **Abstract**: The emergence of Large Language Models (LLMs) has ushered in a transformative paradigm in artificial intelligence, Agentic AI, where intelligent agents exhibit goal-directed autonomy, contextual reasoning, and dynamic multi-agent coordination. This paper provides a systematic review and comparative analysis of leading Agentic AI frameworks, including CrewAI, LangGraph, AutoGen, Semantic Kernel, Agno, Google ADK, and MetaGPT, evaluating their architectural principles, communication mechanisms, memory management, safety guardrails, and alignment with service-oriented computing paradigms. Furthermore, we identify key limitations, emerging trends, and open challenges in the field. To address the issue of agent communication, we conduct an in-depth analysis of protocols such as the Contract Net Protocol (CNP), Agent-to-Agent (A2A), Agent Network Protocol (ANP), and Agora. Our findings not only establish a foundational taxonomy for Agentic AI systems but also propose future research directions to enhance scalability, robustness, and interoperability. This work serves as a comprehensive reference for researchers and practitioners working to advance the next generation of autonomous AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper provides a systematic review and comparative analysis of prominent Agentic AI frameworks, including CrewAI, LangGraph, AutoGen, Semantic Kernel, Agno, Google ADK, and MetaGPT. It examines their architectural principles, communication mechanisms, memory management, safety guardrails, and alignment with service-oriented computing paradigms. The authors also analyze agent communication protocols (CNP, A2A, ANP, Agora) and identify key limitations, emerging trends, and open challenges in the Agentic AI field. The paper aims to establish a foundational taxonomy and propose future research directions to enhance scalability, robustness, and interoperability of these systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The paper offers a broad overview of Agentic AI frameworks, encompassing their architectures, communication protocols, and various functionalities. This is valuable as a central resource for researchers and practitioners.
    *   **Clear Comparative Analysis:** The tabular comparisons (Tables I-V) effectively highlight the differences and similarities between the frameworks, providing a structured understanding of their capabilities and limitations. The use of tables to structure information greatly enhances the ability to quickly identify key features, and compare various approaches.
    *   **Identifies Key Challenges:** The paper successfully identifies critical challenges such as architectural rigidity, limited runtime discovery, code safety concerns, and interoperability gaps. These are significant problems hindering widespread adoption and advancement in the field.
    *   **Well-Defined Research Questions:** The research questions are well-formed and guide the paper's analysis effectively.
    *   **Service Computing Perspective:** The evaluation of the frameworks from a service computing perspective (Table IV) offers a valuable angle, highlighting their readiness for integration into existing enterprise architectures.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Practical Evaluation:** The paper primarily focuses on analyzing the frameworks based on their documentation and design. A more in-depth practical evaluation involving real-world use cases would strengthen the analysis.
    *   **Depth of Protocol Analysis:** While the paper covers multiple communication protocols, the analysis of each protocol might be somewhat superficial. A deeper dive into the intricacies of each protocol, including their strengths and weaknesses in specific scenarios, would be beneficial.
    *   **Lack of Benchmarking:** The paper does not present any quantitative benchmarks or performance comparisons between the frameworks. Including such data would add more weight to the analysis.
    *   **Limited Novelty:** While the paper is a comprehensive survey and analysis, the novelty is moderate. Much of the information presented is based on publicly available documentation and research. The paper synthesizes information effectively, but doesn't offer revolutionary insight.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper provides a valuable consolidated resource for understanding the landscape of Agentic AI frameworks.
    *   It effectively highlights the challenges and opportunities in this emerging field, potentially guiding future research and development efforts.
    *   By analyzing the frameworks from a service computing perspective, the paper bridges the gap between AI and enterprise architectures, potentially facilitating the integration of AI agents into real-world applications.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper is a well-structured and comprehensive overview of Agentic AI frameworks. It successfully synthesizes information from various sources, presents clear comparisons, and identifies key challenges in the field. However, the lack of practical evaluation, limited depth of protocol analysis, and absence of benchmarking data restrict its overall impact and novelty. The core contribution is consolidating and structuring existing knowledge, rather than generating new knowledge *per se*.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### rETF-semiSL: Semi-Supervised Learning for Neural Collapse in Temporal Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10147v1)
- **Authors**: Yuhan Xie, William Cappelletti, Mahsa Shoaran, Pascal Frossard
- **Abstract**: Deep neural networks for time series must capture complex temporal patterns, to effectively represent dynamic data. Self- and semi-supervised learning methods show promising results in pre-training large models, which -- when finetuned for classification -- often outperform their counterparts trained from scratch. Still, the choice of pretext training tasks is often heuristic and their transferability to downstream classification is not granted, thus we propose a novel semi-supervised pre-training strategy to enforce latent representations that satisfy the Neural Collapse phenomenon observed in optimally trained neural classifiers. We use a rotational equiangular tight frame-classifier and pseudo-labeling to pre-train deep encoders with few labeled samples. Furthermore, to effectively capture temporal dynamics while enforcing embedding separability, we integrate generative pretext tasks with our method, and we define a novel sequential augmentation strategy. We show that our method significantly outperforms previous pretext tasks when applied to LSTMs, transformers, and state-space models on three multivariate time series classification datasets. These results highlight the benefit of aligning pre-training objectives with theoretically grounded embedding geometry.
- **Summary**: The paper introduces rETF-semiSL, a novel semi-supervised pre-training strategy for time series classification. It aims to enforce Neural Collapse (NC) in the latent representations of deep encoders, utilizing a rotational equiangular tight frame (rETF) classifier, pseudo-labeling, and generative pretext tasks with a new sequential augmentation method called "forward mixing". The method is tested on LSTM, transformers, and state-space models across three multivariate time series datasets.  Results suggest that rETF-semiSL consistently outperforms established SSL and supervised methods in downstream classification tasks, with improved convergence speed and reduced computational complexity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a novel combination of existing techniques, rather than groundbreaking individual components. Enforcing NC in latent representations is not entirely new, and ETF classifiers have been explored before. However, the paper's primary novelty lies in:

1.  **Integrating NC with semi-supervised learning for time series data:**  Applying NC principles to a semi-supervised pre-training framework specifically designed for time series classification is a valuable contribution.  Most prior work focused on image data.
2.  **The "forward mixing" augmentation technique:** This is a specific and potentially useful time series augmentation method, aiming to preserve temporal dependencies while introducing data diversity. It's a step beyond general data augmentations and specifically tailored to the data type.
3.  **Pseudo-labeling Algorithm**: The paper proposes a custom pseudo-labeling algorithm tailored to work with the ETF structure. This is a novel addition.

The algorithm leverages several existing components such as rotational feature space and rotational balanced feature spaces. However, the combination of those elements and its adaptation to the time-series domain do constitute novel contributions.

**Significance:** The paper demonstrates strong empirical results across several datasets and model architectures. The consistent outperformance compared to baselines (including supervised and other self/semi-supervised methods) suggests that rETF-semiSL is a valuable pre-training technique for time series classification. The analysis and ablation studies also help justify the design choices and identify the key components of the approach. The reduction in computation and better convergence are additional benefits. The code should be released.

**Weaknesses:**

1.  **Incremental improvements:** While the results are positive, the improvement over supervised contrastive learning is not dramatically high. While the improvements are statistically significant, the impact of the paper might be reduced by its fairly incremental nature.
2.  **Limited theoretical justification for forward mixing:** The motivation for forward mixing is intuitive, but a more rigorous theoretical analysis of why it preserves temporal dependencies and improves performance would strengthen the paper.
3.  **Parameters**: The large number of parameters makes reproduction of results more difficult.

**Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the field by encouraging further research into:

*   Applying NC principles to other semi-supervised learning problems, especially in temporal data.
*   Developing more sophisticated time series augmentation techniques that respect temporal dependencies.
*   Investigating the transferability of pre-trained time series representations across different datasets and tasks.

**Justification for the score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of time series classification. The novelty is moderate, primarily in the combination of existing techniques and the introduction of "forward mixing." The empirical results are strong and consistent, suggesting that rETF-semiSL is a valuable pre-training strategy. However, the improvements are not revolutionary. The theoretical justification for some of the design choices could be strengthened.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### LaajMeter: A Framework for LaaJ Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10161v1)
- **Authors**: Gal Amram, Eitan Farchi, Shmulik Froimovich, Raviv Gal, Avi Ziv
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used as evaluators in natural language processing tasks, a paradigm known as LLM-as-a-Judge (LaaJ). While effective in general domains, LaaJs pose significant challenges in domain-specific contexts, where annotated data is scarce and expert evaluation is costly. In such cases, meta-evaluation is often performed using metrics that have not been validated for the specific domain in which they are applied. As a result, it becomes difficult to determine which metrics effectively identify LaaJ quality, and further, what threshold indicates sufficient evaluator performance. In this work, we introduce LaaJMeter, a simulation-based framework for controlled meta-evaluation of LaaJs. LaaJMeter enables engineers to generate synthetic data representing virtual models and judges, allowing systematic analysis of evaluation metrics under realistic conditions. This helps practitioners validate and refine LaaJs for specific evaluation tasks: they can test whether their metrics correctly distinguish between better and worse (virtual) LaaJs, and estimate appropriate thresholds for evaluator adequacy. We demonstrate the utility of LaaJMeter in a code translation task involving a legacy programming language, showing how different metrics vary in sensitivity to evaluator quality. Our results highlight the limitations of common metrics and the importance of principled metric selection. LaaJMeter provides a scalable and extensible solution for assessing LaaJs in low-resource settings, contributing to the broader effort to ensure trustworthy and reproducible evaluation in NLP.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LaaJMeter, a simulation-based framework designed for the meta-evaluation of Large Language Models used as Judges (LaaJs), particularly in low-resource, domain-specific settings where high-quality human-annotated data is scarce. LaaJMeter allows users to generate synthetic data representing virtual models and judges with controlled characteristics, enabling systematic analysis of evaluation metrics. The framework is demonstrated in a code translation use case involving a legacy programming language. The authors evaluate different metrics like t-tests, Kendall's Tau correlation, and ordering experiments to assess their effectiveness in distinguishing between LaaJs of varying quality.  The study highlights the limitations of simple metrics like t-tests and the utility of more robust metrics like Kendall's Tau, even with imprecise model distance estimation.  The ultimate goal is to provide practitioners with a tool to validate and refine LaaJs for specific evaluation tasks and to determine appropriate thresholds for evaluator adequacy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The idea of using simulation for meta-evaluation is not entirely novel; simulation has been used in other areas of NLP and machine learning to assess system behavior under controlled conditions. However, the specific application of a simulation-based framework like LaaJMeter to address the meta-evaluation problem for LLMs as Judges in low-resource, domain-specific contexts is a valuable contribution. The framework provides a practical approach to assess LaaJ quality when expert annotations are limited. The specific design of the virtual models and LaaJs, informed by the authors' practical experience, enhances the realism of the simulations. It takes the existing idea of simulating data to a very particular and complex area of LLM evaluation. The paper does a good job of citing prior work on building domain-specific evaluation datasets, demonstrating the context of the problem they are trying to solve.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a critical and increasingly relevant problem: ensuring the reliability and trustworthiness of LLMs used as evaluators. As LLMs are more widely adopted as judges in various applications, the need for robust meta-evaluation techniques becomes paramount. The framework offers a scalable and extensible solution for assessing LaaJs in low-resource settings, contributing to the broader effort of ensuring trustworthy and reproducible evaluation in NLP.  The ability to systematically analyze evaluation metrics and determine appropriate thresholds is valuable for practitioners who need to validate and refine LaaJs for specific tasks. The use case demonstrating code translation with a legacy language is relevant, as it represents a common scenario where domain expertise and annotated data are limited. The framework also enables reproducibility in LaaJ evaluations, especially in low-resource conditions, by providing a way to create synthetic data that mimics realistic distributions. This is significant because it makes meta-evaluation accessible even without human labeled data.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clearly Defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the challenge of meta-evaluation for LLMs as judges in low-resource contexts.
*   **Practical Framework:** LaaJMeter offers a practical and well-defined simulation-based framework for addressing this challenge.
*   **Realistic Simulation:** The framework generates synthetic data that simulates realistic behaviors of models and LaaJs, informed by the authors' experience.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates different metrics and analyzes their strengths and limitations.
*   **Code Translation Use Case:** The use case demonstrates the utility of the framework in a relevant and challenging domain.
*   **Emphasis on reproducibility** By generating synthetic data, LaaJMeter provides a method to reproduce evaluations without relying on human annotations.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Generalizability:** While the code translation use case is relevant, it is important to acknowledge that the specific design choices for virtual models and LaaJs may need to be adapted for other domains. The effectiveness of LaaJMeter across a wider range of tasks and domains needs to be demonstrated. How would we know if LaaJMeter is successfully simulating a situation that is aligned with the behaviors of real LaaJs?
*   **Metric Dependence:** The evaluation is performed on a simulated environment, so the resulting metrics need to be validated on real LaaJs as well to show that the simulation corresponds to the real world effectively. The paper doesn't make it clear that the metrics found suitable in the simulation correlate well with human labeled data for those same LaaJs. This is a real concern and weakness.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution to the field of LLM evaluation by introducing a practical and scalable framework for meta-evaluation in low-resource settings. While the idea of using simulation is not entirely novel, the application to the LaaJ meta-evaluation problem and the detailed design of the framework are significant. The limitations regarding generalizability and the need for further validation on real-world datasets should be addressed, however, the work offers a valuable contribution and could have real impact.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Pruning Long Chain-of-Thought of Large Reasoning Models via Small-Scale Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10164v1)
- **Authors**: Bin Hong, Jiayu Liu, Zhenya Huang, Kai Zhang, Mengdi Zhang
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) have demonstrated strong performance on complex tasks through long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. However, their lengthy outputs increase computational costs and may lead to overthinking, raising challenges in balancing reasoning effectiveness and efficiency. Current methods for efficient reasoning often compromise reasoning quality or require extensive resources. This paper investigates efficient methods to reduce the generation length of LRMs. We analyze generation path distributions and filter generated trajectories through difficulty estimation. Subsequently, we analyze the convergence behaviors of the objectives of various preference optimization methods under a Bradley-Terry loss based framework. Based on the analysis, we propose Length Controlled Preference Optimization (LCPO) that directly balances the implicit reward related to NLL loss. LCPO can effectively learn length preference with limited data and training. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach significantly reduces the average output length by over 50\% across multiple benchmarks while maintaining the reasoning performance. Our work highlights the potential for computationally efficient approaches in guiding LRMs toward efficient reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Pruning Long Chain-of-Thought of Large Reasoning Models via Small-Scale Preference Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of excessive output length in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) using Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. The authors propose Length Controlled Preference Optimization (LCPO), a method that balances the implicit reward related to Negative Log-Likelihood (NLL) loss to encourage shorter, more efficient reasoning. LCPO analyzes generation path distributions, filters generated trajectories through difficulty estimation, and applies preference optimization. The method aims to reduce computational costs and mitigate overthinking. The authors analyze the convergence behaviors of various preference optimization methods under a Bradley-Terry loss framework and show that LCPO can effectively learn length preferences with limited data and training. Experiments demonstrate significant reductions in output length while maintaining reasoning performance across multiple benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Relevance:** The paper tackles a crucial issue in LRM research – the trade-off between reasoning effectiveness and efficiency. Long CoT outputs are computationally expensive and can lead to overthinking.
    *   **Methodological Contribution:** LCPO provides a computationally efficient approach for guiding LRMs toward efficient reasoning. The analysis of generation path distributions and difficulty estimation for data filtering is a valuable contribution.
    *   **Theoretical Foundation:** The convergence analysis under the Bradley-Terry framework offers theoretical insights into preference optimization methods and the impact of NLL loss on length preference.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks (MATH-500, GSM8K, etc.) and model sizes (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B and 7B) demonstrate the effectiveness of LCPO in reducing output length while maintaining reasoning performance.
    *   **Practical Applicability:** The method's low data and training requirements make it practically applicable and computationally efficient compared to resource-intensive RL-based approaches.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Difficulty Estimation Heuristic:** The difficulty estimation method relies on a coarse, heuristic approach based on the accuracy score of generated samples. It could be improved by using more sophisticated techniques. The reliance on pass@1 can be noisy.
    *   **Limited Diversity in Training Data:** The training set is based on math tasks, which might limit the generalizability of the approach to other reasoning domains.
    *   **Focus on One Model Type:** Evaluation is primarily focused on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen models. Testing with other LRM architectures would further strengthen the findings. The models themselves are distillations, so this limits impact somewhat, as they are unlikely to be used as production models.
    *   **OOD performance decrease.** The MMLU results show that while there is still length reduction OOD, the accuracy decreased slightly in both models.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper's novelty lies in its efficient preference optimization approach for controlling output length in LRMs, particularly its analysis of NLL loss and the introduction of LCPO.
    *   The findings have implications for LRM research by highlighting the potential for computationally efficient methods to improve reasoning efficiency without compromising performance.
    *   The convergence analysis offers valuable insights into preference optimization methods under the Bradley-Terry framework.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    While the paper makes valuable contributions to addressing a significant problem in LRM research, it has limitations. The difficulty estimation heuristic and the focus on math tasks could be improved, and the evaluation could be expanded to include more diverse model architectures and task domains. The impact on OOD performance suggests that length reduction comes at a cost to accuracy in settings outside the training domain.

**Score: 7**

The paper presents a solid contribution to efficient reasoning in LRMs with theoretical grounding and practical improvements. While there are some notable limitations (difficulty estimation, the lack of model diversity in evaluation, limited training data, and OOD performance degradation), the significance of the problem and the effectiveness of the proposed LCPO method warrant a positive assessment.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Benchmark-Driven Selection of AI: Evidence from DeepSeek-R1
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10173v1)
- **Authors**: Petr Spelda, Vit Stritecky
- **Abstract**: Evaluation of reasoning language models gained importance after it was observed that they can combine their existing capabilities into novel traces of intermediate steps before task completion and that the traces can sometimes help them to generalize better than past models. As reasoning becomes the next scaling dimension of large language models, careful study of their capabilities in critical tasks is needed. We show that better performance is not always caused by test-time algorithmic improvements or model sizes but also by using impactful benchmarks as curricula for learning. We call this benchmark-driven selection of AI and show its effects on DeepSeek-R1 using our sequential decision-making problem from Humanity's Last Exam. Steering development of AI by impactful benchmarks trades evaluation for learning and makes novelty of test tasks key for measuring generalization capabilities of reasoning models. Consequently, some benchmarks could be seen as curricula for training rather than unseen test sets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of benchmarks on the development of AI models, specifically Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors introduce the term "benchmark-driven selection of AI," arguing that using impactful benchmarks as training curricula can influence model performance, potentially at the expense of genuine generalization. They examine the effects of this phenomenon on DeepSeek-R1, using a sequential decision-making problem from Humanity's Last Exam (HLE) as a case study. They show that performance gains in later versions of DeepSeek-R1 are partially attributable to the inclusion of HLE-like tasks in the model's training, rather than solely to algorithmic improvements or increased model size.  The paper emphasizes the importance of evaluating models on truly unseen tasks to accurately assess their generalization capabilities and suggests that some benchmarks might inadvertently function as training curricula.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Concept:** The concept of "benchmark-driven selection of AI" is a valuable contribution. It highlights a potential bias in how we evaluate and develop AI models, urging researchers to be more mindful of benchmark influence. The coining of this term provides a useful framing for future discussions.
    *   **Empirical Evidence:** The case study with DeepSeek-R1 and the HLE task provides empirical support for the authors' argument. The performance drop of the original DeepSeek-R1 compared to later versions after the HLE release strengthens the claim that benchmark-driven selection is occurring.
    *   **Relevance to the Field:**  The paper addresses a crucial challenge in the field of LLMs. As models become increasingly powerful, it is essential to understand whether improvements stem from genuine intelligence or are simply artifacts of training on specific benchmarks.
    *   **Clear Argument and Methodology:** The paper presents a clear argument supported by well-defined evaluation criteria and a relatively sound methodology involving human judgement of model responses.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** While the case study is informative, it is based on a single task and model.  Generalizing the findings to all benchmarks and LLMs requires further investigation across diverse tasks, models, and benchmarks.
    *   **Statistical Rigor:** Although Bayesian credible intervals are used, the sample size (N=64) is relatively small. This limits the statistical power of the analysis and increases the uncertainty surrounding the reported performance differences.  The paper could benefit from a more robust statistical analysis.
    *   **Alternative Explanations:** The performance difference between DeepSeek-R1 and later versions could be influenced by other factors not fully explored, such as subtle changes in the training data or optimization algorithms, independent of the HLE task.  While the authors address token count, more exhaustive exploration of confounding factors would strengthen the argument.
    *   **Operationalization of "Unseen Tasks":** The paper stresses the importance of "unseen tasks." It's unclear what constitutes a truly unseen task, given the vast pre-training datasets used for LLMs. A clearer discussion around how to construct and evaluate such tasks would be valuable.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper identifies and articulates a critical issue (benchmark-driven selection) that has important implications for AI development and evaluation. It offers a valuable perspective on interpreting performance gains in LLMs and encourages a more nuanced approach to benchmarking. It highlights that using benchmarks as a training process should be viewed differently compared to true evaluations.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning this paper a score of **7**.

*   While the paper makes a valuable contribution by highlighting the potential pitfalls of relying solely on benchmark performance, its scope is somewhat limited, and certain aspects of its methodology could be more robust.
*   The case study is compelling, but further empirical validation across a wider range of models and tasks is needed to solidify the conclusions.
*   The novelty lies in the framing of the "benchmark-driven selection" concept and its potential to influence model development, prompting a re-evaluation of how benchmarks are perceived.
*   The paper is timely and relevant, addressing an increasingly important challenge in the field of LLMs.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Efficient Forward-Only Data Valuation for Pretrained LLMs and VLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10180v1)
- **Authors**: Wenlong Deng, Jiaming Zhang, Qi Zeng, Christos Thrampoulidis, Boying Gong, Xiaoxiao Li
- **Abstract**: Quantifying the influence of individual training samples is essential for enhancing the transparency and accountability of large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs). However, existing data valuation methods often rely on Hessian information or model retraining, making them computationally prohibitive for billion-parameter models. In this work, we introduce For-Value, a forward-only data valuation framework that enables scalable and efficient influence estimation for both LLMs and VLMs. By leveraging the rich representations of modern foundation models, For-Value computes influence scores using a simple closed-form expression based solely on a single forward pass, thereby eliminating the need for costly gradient computations. Our theoretical analysis demonstrates that For-Value accurately estimates per-sample influence by capturing alignment in hidden representations and prediction errors between training and validation samples. Extensive experiments show that For-Value matches or outperforms gradient-based baselines in identifying impactful fine-tuning examples and effectively detecting mislabeled data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces For-Value, a novel and efficient forward-only data valuation framework for pretrained Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs). Unlike existing data valuation methods that rely on computationally expensive Hessian information or model retraining, For-Value leverages the rich representations of modern foundation models to compute influence scores using a simple closed-form expression based on a single forward pass. The paper provides theoretical justification for the approach, demonstrating that For-Value accurately estimates per-sample influence by capturing alignment in hidden representations and prediction errors between training and validation samples. Extensive experiments on various LLM and VLM tasks demonstrate that For-Value matches or outperforms gradient-based baselines in identifying impactful fine-tuning examples and effectively detecting mislabeled data while being significantly more computationally efficient.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its forward-only approach to data valuation for large models. Existing methods typically require gradient computations or model retraining, making them impractical for billion-parameter models. By leveraging hidden representations and formulating a closed-form expression, For-Value significantly reduces the computational burden. The theoretical justification linking representation alignment and prediction error alignment to influence is also a valuable contribution.  However, the core idea of leveraging pre-trained representations isn't entirely new. Several studies have explored the usefulness of representations for various downstream tasks. The novelty resides in *how* these representations are used within the context of data valuation and the resulting efficiency gains. The simplification to focus on 'seen vocabularies' to improve efficiency is a clever optimization trick, but doesn't fundamentally alter the algorithmic approach.

**Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to make data valuation more accessible and practical for large models. By enabling efficient influence estimation, For-Value can be used to improve the transparency and accountability of LLMs and VLMs, identify noisy or irrelevant training data, and potentially improve model performance.  The ability to perform data valuation *without* requiring access to gradients or model fine-tuning is particularly significant, given the increasing prevalence of closed-source LLMs and APIs. However, the current experiments predominantly focus on somewhat standard downstream tasks. Demonstrating the impact of For-Value on more complex, real-world scenarios (e.g., bias detection, fairness auditing, factuality improvement of LLM outputs) would further strengthen its significance. The improvement in AUC and recall, while consistent, are often incremental, meaning that although computationally efficient, there isn't a huge leap in performance accuracy compared to the baseline methods.

**Strengths:**

*   **Computational Efficiency:** The forward-only approach offers a significant speedup compared to gradient-based methods.
*   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides a theoretical analysis to support the effectiveness of For-Value.
*   **Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of For-Value on a variety of tasks and models.
*   **Practical Applicability:** The method can be applied to closed-source LLMs without access to gradients.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental improvements:**  While the authors have compared For-value with relevant baselines and the results show that their method is consistently competitive, For-Value does not substantially outperform the SOTA methods.
*   **Limited Focus on Real-World Impact:** While the paper addresses model influence in tasks like translation and math, it could further benefit from demonstrating real-world influence in applications such as misinformation detection.
*   **Dependence on Hidden Representation Quality:** The performance of For-Value is inherently tied to the quality of the hidden representations produced by the pretrained model. This dependency isn't thoroughly addressed, potentially limiting its applicability to certain models or domains.
*   **Assumption 2:** The assumption of "Distinct Input" which states that no training input is identical to the valuation input is not valid in every domain.

**Justification for Score:**

For-Value offers a valuable contribution to the field by providing a scalable and efficient solution for data valuation in large models.  The theoretical justification and extensive empirical validation support its effectiveness. However, the incremental improvements in performance and the limited demonstration of real-world impact prevent it from achieving a higher score. Also, the reliance of the method on high-quality pre-trained representations and the Distinct Input assumption, without detailed discussion or solutions, somewhat limits it.

- **Classification**: cs.CL

### PakBBQ: A Culturally Adapted Bias Benchmark for QA
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10186v1)
- **Authors**: Abdullah Hashmat, Muhammad Arham Mirza, Agha Ali Raza
- **Abstract**: With the widespread adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) across various applications, it is empirical to ensure their fairness across all user communities. However, most LLMs are trained and evaluated on Western centric data, with little attention paid to low-resource languages and regional contexts. To address this gap, we introduce PakBBQ, a culturally and regionally adapted extension of the original Bias Benchmark for Question Answering (BBQ) dataset. PakBBQ comprises over 214 templates, 17180 QA pairs across 8 categories in both English and Urdu, covering eight bias dimensions including age, disability, appearance, gender, socio-economic status, religious, regional affiliation, and language formality that are relevant in Pakistan. We evaluate multiple multilingual LLMs under both ambiguous and explicitly disambiguated contexts, as well as negative versus non negative question framings. Our experiments reveal (i) an average accuracy gain of 12\% with disambiguation, (ii) consistently stronger counter bias behaviors in Urdu than in English, and (iii) marked framing effects that reduce stereotypical responses when questions are posed negatively. These findings highlight the importance of contextualized benchmarks and simple prompt engineering strategies for bias mitigation in low resource settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PakBBQ, a culturally adapted bias benchmark for question answering (QA) tailored to the Pakistani context.  It builds upon the original BBQ dataset, modifying and adding templates to capture biases specific to Pakistani society, covering dimensions like regional affiliation, sect, and language formality, in addition to common biases like gender and age. The authors evaluate several multilingual LLMs on PakBBQ in both English and Urdu, analyzing accuracy, bias disparities, and performance across different contexts (ambiguous vs. disambiguated) and template types. The findings reveal that: (1) disambiguation significantly improves accuracy, (2) Urdu exhibits stronger counter-bias behaviors, and (3) negatively framed questions reduce stereotypical responses.  The paper highlights the importance of contextualized benchmarks for bias mitigation in low-resource settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by creating a culturally specific bias benchmark for QA.  While the methodology borrows from other adaptation efforts like KO-BBQ, the contextualization to Pakistan's complex social, linguistic, and religious landscape is novel and important. The inclusion of Urdu and the analysis of formality biases adds another layer of originality. The experiments with Urdu responses have been underdone.
*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it addresses a critical gap in AI fairness research: the lack of benchmarks tailored to non-Western, low-resource contexts. Most existing bias benchmarks are Western-centric, leading to potentially undetected and amplified biases in other regions. By developing PakBBQ, the authors provide a much-needed resource for evaluating and mitigating biases in LLMs deployed in Pakistan.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Rigorous adaptation methodology, building upon existing work while incorporating culturally relevant biases.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation of multiple LLMs across different contexts and languages.
    *   The finding that simple interventions, like disambiguation and negative framing, can significantly reduce bias is useful and practical.
    *   The inclusion of Urdu, a relatively low-resource language, expands the scope of bias evaluation beyond English-centric settings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the authors describe the data collection and template modification processes, more detail on the validation and annotation of culturally relevant bias could enhance the rigor. The Fleiss' Kappa of 0.2 is quite low, raising questions about inter-annotator agreement, though the relevance score helps mitigate this.
    *   The analysis could be more nuanced.  For example, are there specific bias categories where the performance gap between English and Urdu is particularly pronounced? Exploring the intersection of different bias dimensions could also offer valuable insights.
    *   The paper acknowledges limitations, such as the focus on only Urdu and English, and could offer deeper discussion of the potential ethical implications of exposing and quantifying harmful stereotypes, even for evaluation purposes.

*   **Impact:** The paper's potential impact lies in its contribution to fairer and more equitable AI systems. By providing a culturally specific bias benchmark, PakBBQ enables more accurate assessment and mitigation of biases in LLMs deployed in Pakistan, preventing the perpetuation of harmful stereotypes. The findings also have broader implications for developing culturally sensitive bias benchmarks in other underrepresented regions.
*   **Areas for Improvement:** The paper would benefit from more granular analysis of results across bias categories and models. A deeper dive into the reasons behind performance variations would increase the value of the paper. Also, the impact of translation needs to be addressed with more detail.
    *   *Improve inter-annotator agreement validation or find another means of more robust assessment*

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper offers a solid contribution to a growing area of concern around AI biases in diverse cultural settings, warranting a score in the higher end of the spectrum. It demonstrates the critical need for adaptation in bias assessment, and it backs up those claims with experimental data that is well represented. While it builds on previous research and could use more nuanced analysis and validation of results, the work represents a meaningful advance in fairness evaluation for LLMs and offers practical insights for bias mitigation. The inclusion of Urdu language and cultural context makes this paper unique and important.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompt-Response Semantic Divergence Metrics for Faithfulness Hallucination and Misalignment Detection in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10192v1)
- **Authors**: Igor Halperin
- **Abstract**: The proliferation of Large Language Models (LLMs) is challenged by hallucinations, critical failure modes where models generate non-factual, nonsensical or unfaithful text. This paper introduces Semantic Divergence Metrics (SDM), a novel lightweight framework for detecting Faithfulness Hallucinations -- events of severe deviations of LLMs responses from input contexts. We focus on a specific implementation of these LLM errors, {confabulations, defined as responses that are arbitrary and semantically misaligned with the user's query. Existing methods like Semantic Entropy test for arbitrariness by measuring the diversity of answers to a single, fixed prompt. Our SDM framework improves upon this by being more prompt-aware: we test for a deeper form of arbitrariness by measuring response consistency not only across multiple answers but also across multiple, semantically-equivalent paraphrases of the original prompt. Methodologically, our approach uses joint clustering on sentence embeddings to create a shared topic space for prompts and answers. A heatmap of topic co-occurances between prompts and responses can be viewed as a quantified two-dimensional visualization of the user-machine dialogue. We then compute a suite of information-theoretic metrics to measure the semantic divergence between prompts and responses. Our practical score, $\mathcal{S}_H$, combines the Jensen-Shannon divergence and Wasserstein distance to quantify this divergence, with a high score indicating a Faithfulness hallucination. Furthermore, we identify the KL divergence KL(Answer $||$ Prompt) as a powerful indicator of \textbf{Semantic Exploration}, a key signal for distinguishing different generative behaviors. These metrics are further combined into the Semantic Box, a diagnostic framework for classifying LLM response types, including the dangerous, confident confabulation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Semantic Divergence Metrics (SDM), a novel framework for detecting "faithfulness hallucinations" (semantic misalignment between LLM responses and input prompts) in large language models. The framework focuses on confabulations, defined as arbitrary and semantically misaligned responses. SDM improves on existing methods like Semantic Entropy by being more prompt-aware, measuring response consistency across multiple paraphrases of the original prompt. It uses joint clustering on sentence embeddings to create a shared topic space, visualizes prompt-response co-occurrences as a heatmap, and computes information-theoretic metrics (Jensen-Shannon divergence, Wasserstein distance, KL divergence) to quantify semantic divergence. A practical score, SH, combining JSD and Wasserstein distance, indicates faithfulness hallucination risk. KL divergence KL(Answer || Prompt) identifies Semantic Exploration, differentiating generative behaviors. The Semantic Box, a diagnostic framework, classifies LLM response types (including dangerous confident confabulation). The authors argue this is a principled method for real-time detecting faithfulness hallucinations and semantic misalignment in black-box LLMs. Experiments validate the approach, showing it distinguishes response types and captures a "stability gradient" in prompt ambiguity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates moderate novelty.  While Semantic Entropy (SE) is the foundational baseline, the core innovation is the prompt-aware nature of SDM. This includes:
    *   Using multiple paraphrased prompts instead of just one.
    *   Employing joint embedding clustering across prompts and answers to create a shared topic space.
    *   Introducing a suite of information-theoretic metrics beyond simple entropy.
    *   Developing the "Semantic Box" as a diagnostic tool.

    However, several components leverage existing techniques (e.g., hierarchical clustering, JSD, Wasserstein distance). The real novelty lies in *how* these techniques are combined and applied in a prompt-aware manner to tackle the specific problem of detecting faithfulness hallucinations.

*   **Significance:** The significance is considerable.  Hallucination detection is a critical challenge for the reliable deployment of LLMs. The SDM framework offers:
    *   A lightweight approach suitable for real-time monitoring.
    *   A theoretically grounded framework based on semantic divergence.
    *   A diagnostic approach that goes beyond simple binary classification, providing insights into *why* a model might be hallucinating.
    *   The "Semantic Box" offers a practical way to categorize model behavior, which can guide mitigation strategies (e.g., fine-tuning, prompt engineering).

    The paper's emphasis on *faithfulness* hallucinations, as opposed to factuality (which requires external knowledge), is valuable as it focuses on a particularly insidious form of error where the LLM *appears* to be aligned but is actually generating nonsensical or contextually irrelevant content. The exploration of both exploration and instability is interesting.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong theoretical foundation:** The information-theoretic rationale is well-articulated.
    *   **Comprehensive methodology:**  The multi-stage process (data generation, embedding, clustering, metric calculation) is clearly described.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The experiments provide evidence of the framework's effectiveness in controlled settings.  The qualitative heatmap analysis is particularly insightful.
    *   **Clear presentation:** The paper is well-written and structured, making the complex concepts accessible.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** Focus on a key limitation of black-box LLMs makes it highly relevant for deployment.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Complexity:**  The time complexity dominated by hierarchical clustering (O(S^2)) could be a bottleneck for very large prompts or conversations. The paper could benefit from exploring more scalable clustering algorithms.
    *   **Dependence on Sentence Embeddings:** The framework's performance depends heavily on the quality of the sentence embeddings.  While the authors used Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B, exploring other embedding models and their impact on SDM's performance would strengthen the analysis.
    *   **Limited Scale of Experiments:** While the experiments are insightful, the prompts are relatively short. Testing the SDM framework on longer documents and more complex RAG scenarios would be valuable.
    *   **Calibration Requirement:** The paper acknowledges the need for calibration data in practical deployments.  However, it doesn't offer concrete guidance on how to efficiently acquire or generate this calibration data. The self-calibrating framework is proposed but not yet demonstrated.

*   **Potential Influence:** The SDM framework has the potential to influence the development of more robust and reliable LLM evaluation systems. The emphasis on prompt-awareness and diagnostic capabilities could inspire new approaches to hallucination detection. The "Semantic Box" provides a practical framework for categorizing model behavior that could be adopted by practitioners. Furthermore, the clear delineation between faithfulness and factuality hallucinations is useful for framing the problem.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of LLM reliability by introducing a prompt-aware framework for detecting faithfulness hallucinations. While the approach relies on existing techniques, the specific combination and application are novel and well-justified. The strengths of the paper (strong theoretical foundation, comprehensive methodology, empirical validation, practical relevance) outweigh its weaknesses (computational complexity, dependence on sentence embeddings, limited scale of experiments, calibration requirement). The potential influence on future research and practical applications is significant.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### B-repLer: Semantic B-rep Latent Editor using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10201v1)
- **Authors**: Yilin Liu, Niladri Shekhar Dutt, Changjian Li, Niloy J. Mitra
- **Abstract**: Multimodal large language models (mLLMs), trained in a mixed modal setting as a universal model, have been shown to compete with or even outperform many specialized algorithms for imaging and graphics tasks. As demonstrated across many applications, mLLMs' ability to jointly process image and text data makes them suitable for zero-shot applications or efficient fine-tuning towards specialized tasks. However, they have had limited success in 3D analysis and editing tasks. This is due to both the lack of suitable (annotated) 3D data as well as the idiosyncrasies of 3D representations. In this paper, we investigate whether mLLMs can be adapted to support high-level editing of Boundary Representation (B-rep) CAD objects. B-reps remain the industry-standard for precisely encoding engineering objects, but are challenging as the representation is fragile (i.e. can easily lead to invalid CAD objects) and no publicly available data source exists with semantically-annotated B-reps or CAD construction history. We present B-repLer as a finetuned mLLM that can understand text prompts and make semantic edits on given B-Reps to produce valid outputs. We enable this via a novel multimodal architecture, specifically designed to handle B-rep models, and demonstrate how existing CAD tools, in conjunction with mLLMs, can be used to automatically generate the required reasoning dataset, without relying on external annotations. We extensively evaluate B-repLer and demonstrate several text-based B-rep edits of various complexity, which were not previously possible.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "B-repLer: Semantic B-rep Latent Editor using Large Language Models."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces B-repLer, a novel approach for editing Boundary Representation (B-rep) CAD models using natural language instructions. Unlike previous methods that rely on image or point cloud representations, B-repLer directly operates on the B-rep latent space. The method utilizes a two-stage architecture: 1) an mLLM (Qwen2.5 VL 7B) is finetuned to localize the region of interest in the CAD model based on the user's prompt and generate a detailed (low-level) geometric editing instruction; and 2) a bespoke Transformer is trained in the B-rep latent space to perform the actual modification, conditioned on the user prompt, the bounding box, and intermediate rendered images. The paper also contributes BrepEDIT-10K, a new dataset of paired B-rep models with text instructions for training and evaluating CAD editing methods. Extensive evaluations and ablation studies demonstrate the effectiveness and versatility of B-repLer.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its **direct manipulation of B-rep models using text instructions**, avoiding the typical intermediate representations like images or point clouds. This is significant because B-reps are the industry standard for CAD, but are notoriously difficult to work with due to their fragility and lack of semantically annotated datasets. Finetuning an mLLM for localizing user instructions in CAD models by linking an mLLM to B-rep latent space is a significant achievement. The approach is also novel in the specific architecture, including the two-stage process that explicitly breaks the editing problem into localization and modification steps. Further, the automatic generation of BrepEDIT-10k is key.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical and challenging problem in CAD: enabling users to easily edit complex models using intuitive instructions. B-repLer makes CAD editing more accessible, potentially reducing the barrier to entry for non-expert users and improving the efficiency of experienced designers. It directly addresses the challenge that existing datasets have not been available that explicitly connects semantic intructions to edits. The use of mLLMs to interpret high-level instructions and map them to concrete B-rep modifications demonstrates significant progress in using AI for design tasks. The release of the BrepEDIT-10K dataset will also have a significant impact on the field, by enabling further research in text-driven CAD editing.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Direct manipulation of B-rep models, maintaining validity and precision
    *   Effective two-stage architecture, combining the reasoning abilities of mLLMs with a specialized Transformer for latent space modification.
    *   Novel methodology for automatic generation of a paired B-rep model editing dataset.
    *   Comprehensive experimental results and ablation studies demonstrating the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   The B-rep models and code is released which will allow other researchers to immediately build on the method.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method's limitation to single-step editing operations and B-rep face deletion only. While the authors acknowledge that real-world CAD modeling often involves multi-step processes.
    *   The modest Intersection over Union (IoU) scores for bounding box prediction (0.51 and 0.31).
    *   There is still the need for an underlying dataset and associated B-rep latent editor.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could significantly influence the field of CAD by:
    *   Inspiring new research into text-driven CAD editing techniques.
    *   Providing a strong baseline for future methods in the area.
    *   Enabling more intuitive and accessible CAD editing tools for users.
    *   Accelerating the integration of AI into the design process.

*   **Justification of Score:** While the paper's results and two-stage architecture are promising, the dependence on delete face operations, the inherent complexity of multi-step edits, as well as the relatively modest IoU scores mean there are still significant limitations. However, the novel pipeline, dataset, and B-rep specific processing mean this paper represents a significant advance in the field.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Using Large Language Models to Measure Symptom Severity in Patients At Risk for Schizophrenia
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10226v1)
- **Authors**: Andrew X. Chen, Guillermo Horga, Sean Escola
- **Abstract**: Patients who are at clinical high risk (CHR) for schizophrenia need close monitoring of their symptoms to inform appropriate treatments. The Brief Psychiatric Rating Scale (BPRS) is a validated, commonly used research tool for measuring symptoms in patients with schizophrenia and other psychotic disorders; however, it is not commonly used in clinical practice as it requires a lengthy structured interview. Here, we utilize large language models (LLMs) to predict BPRS scores from clinical interview transcripts in 409 CHR patients from the Accelerating Medicines Partnership Schizophrenia (AMP-SCZ) cohort. Despite the interviews not being specifically structured to measure the BPRS, the zero-shot performance of the LLM predictions compared to the true assessment (median concordance: 0.84, ICC: 0.73) approaches human inter- and intra-rater reliability. We further demonstrate that LLMs have substantial potential to improve and standardize the assessment of CHR patients via their accuracy in assessing the BPRS in foreign languages (median concordance: 0.88, ICC: 0.70), and integrating longitudinal information in a one-shot or few-shot learning approach.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Using Large Language Models to Measure Symptom Severity in Patients At Risk for Schizophrenia".

**Summary**

This paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) to predict Brief Psychiatric Rating Scale (BPRS) scores from clinical interview transcripts of patients at clinical high risk (CHR) for schizophrenia. The study uses the AMP-SCZ cohort data and demonstrates that LLMs can predict BPRS scores with accuracy approaching human inter- and intra-rater reliability, even in a zero-shot setting (without task-specific training). The study also demonstrates the LLM's ability to handle foreign language transcripts and incorporate longitudinal information to improve prediction accuracy.  The results suggest that LLMs have the potential to improve and standardize symptom assessment in CHR populations.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Novelty and Significance:*

The paper has *moderate* novelty. While the use of LLMs in mental health is growing, this paper presents a compelling application for a specific high-risk population (CHR for schizophrenia) and a specific assessment tool (BPRS). Previous work has explored LLMs for depression and anxiety, but the application to psychosis risk represents a valuable extension. The investigation of multilingual capabilities and longitudinal integration further adds to its novelty. However, the core methodology is not groundbreaking, relying on established LLMs and standard metrics. It also lacks any formal comparison to other comparable methods.

*Strengths:*

*   **Real-world data:** Using a large, real-world clinical dataset (AMP-SCZ) enhances the practical relevance of the findings.
*   **Zero-shot learning:** Demonstrating decent performance in a zero-shot setting makes the approach more practical, as it bypasses the need for extensive task-specific training data.
*   **Multilingual capability:**  Showing that LLMs can process foreign language transcripts without explicit retraining is a significant advantage.
*   **Longitudinal Integration:** Exploring how longitudinal data can improve performance is a valuable contribution, suggesting potential for personalized monitoring.
*   **Clear methodology:** The methods section is reasonably clear, allowing for potential replication.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Clinical Utility Assessment:** The paper focuses on technical accuracy, but it *lacks a detailed discussion of the potential clinical impact* of using LLMs for BPRS assessment.  How would this tool be integrated into clinical workflows? What are the potential benefits for patients and clinicians?
*   **Over-Reliance on Zero-Shot:** While the zero-shot aspect is useful, the paper doesn't explore fine-tuning or task-specific training, which could potentially yield significant performance gains.  A comparison of zero-shot vs. fine-tuned performance would strengthen the paper.
*   **Limited Error Analysis:** The paper briefly mentions subscores with low concordance, but a more detailed error analysis could provide valuable insights into the limitations of the LLM and guide future improvements. What types of errors does it make? What specific language patterns or interview characteristics are associated with these errors?
*   **Vague Model Details:**  While the model used is mentioned, details about prompt engineering beyond including the BPRS manual are unclear. Replicability would be improved by offering clearer details regarding prompt structure.
*   **Lack of Formal Comparison:** The paper only compares the LLM results to human rater reliability data. While this is a valuable benchmark, it would be even stronger to compare the LLM's performance against simpler machine learning models or established natural language processing techniques to truly demonstrate its superiority.
*   **Lack of Statistical Significance:** Many claims, while interesting, are not backed with adequate statistical significance testing. This detracts from the credibility of the work.

*Significance:*

The work suggests a potential avenue for automating and standardizing symptom assessment, which could be particularly useful in resource-constrained settings. If the tool can show clinical utility in improving patient care, it could have a significant positive impact on the field.

**Overall Score and Justification**

Score: 5/10

Rationale:

The paper has potential, but falls short of being highly innovative. The application of LLMs to predict BPRS scores in CHR patients is worthwhile, and the multilingual and longitudinal aspects add value. However, the study lacks a comprehensive assessment of clinical utility, relies heavily on zero-shot learning without exploring fine-tuning, provides limited error analysis, and lacks a formal comparison to other models. The lack of statistically significant claims further detracts from the paper's credibility. While promising, it requires more robust validation and clinical relevance assessment to achieve higher impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### Can Transformers Break Encryption Schemes via In-Context Learning?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10235v1)
- **Authors**: Jathin Korrapati, Patrick Mendoza, Aditya Tomar, Abein Abraham
- **Abstract**: In-context learning (ICL) has emerged as a powerful capability of transformer-based language models, enabling them to perform tasks by conditioning on a small number of examples presented at inference time, without any parameter updates. Prior work has shown that transformers can generalize over simple function classes like linear functions, decision trees, even neural networks, purely from context, focusing on numerical or symbolic reasoning over underlying well-structured functions. Instead, we propose a novel application of ICL into the domain of cryptographic function learning, specifically focusing on ciphers such as mono-alphabetic substitution and Vigen\`ere ciphers, two classes of private-key encryption schemes. These ciphers involve a fixed but hidden bijective mapping between plain text and cipher text characters. Given a small set of (cipher text, plain text) pairs, the goal is for the model to infer the underlying substitution and decode a new cipher text word. This setting poses a structured inference challenge, which is well-suited for evaluating the inductive biases and generalization capabilities of transformers under the ICL paradigm. Code is available at https://github.com/adistomar/CS182-project.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the ability of transformer-based language models (LLMs) to learn and break encryption schemes via in-context learning (ICL).  Instead of traditional numerical or symbolic reasoning, the authors focus on cryptographic function learning, specifically mono-alphabetic substitution and Vigenère ciphers.  The study investigates whether models can generalize decryption behavior for unseen keys by providing ciphertext-plaintext pairs as in-context examples.  The authors compare the models' performance against established cryptanalytic attacks like frequency analysis. They found that transformers can indeed learn these ciphers to a degree, especially the simpler mono-alphabetic substitution cipher, and show some ability to generalize beyond the training distribution.  However, performance degrades with increasing complexity, particularly with variable-length Vigenère ciphers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of ICL to the domain of cryptography is somewhat novel, although neural cryptanalysis itself isn't a new field. The approach of using transformers' in-context learning ability *without* specific cryptanalytic training data is an interesting angle. Connecting this to the broader literature on function class learning (Garg et al., 2022) provides a valuable theoretical grounding. The novelty lies more in the *framing* and experimental setup, rather than a fundamentally new algorithm or technique.

*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate.  The paper demonstrates that transformers *can* learn basic ciphers via ICL, which is interesting from a meta-learning perspective.  It provides a case study of structured inference. However, the ciphers studied are quite simple. Their limitations in the face of complexity also highlight the challenges of using transformers for more sophisticated tasks. The paper doesn't break any existing encryption systems, nor does it suggest a radically new approach to cryptanalysis. The value lies more in the exploratory insights into the capabilities and limitations of ICL when applied to a structured inference problem.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem framing and methodology.
    *   Well-defined experimental setup with appropriate baselines (naive decoder and frequency analysis).
    *   Analysis of generalization abilities, including out-of-distribution performance.
    *   Connections to existing literature on ICL and function class learning.
    *   Reproducibility through code availability.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited to relatively simple classical ciphers. Real-world cryptography is significantly more complex.
    *   The model used is relatively small. Scaling up the model may result in better performance.
    *   The paper doesn't provide a deep theoretical explanation of *why* transformers are able to learn these ciphers. It's more of an empirical exploration.
    *   The performance is not very robust, and the authors were unable to achieve high accuracy for variable key length Vignere ciphers. This suggests that the problem may be more complex than the transformer is capable of handling.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper may inspire further research into using transformers for other structured inference tasks.  It could also prompt investigation into the inductive biases that enable ICL. Its main contribution is highlighting the strengths and weaknesses of LLMs in solving structural/logic problems by using cryptography as a testbed.

**Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a moderately novel application of ICL with a well-defined experimental setup. However, the limitations in terms of the ciphers studied, the lack of a deeper theoretical explanation, and modest impact, limit its significance. While it offers valuable insights, it doesn't represent a breakthrough. It is an interesting case study, but requires further work to truly be valuable to the field.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Pruning and Malicious Injection: A Retraining-Free Backdoor Attack on Transformer Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10243v1)
- **Authors**: Taibiao Zhao, Mingxuan Sun, Hao Wang, Xiaobing Chen, Xiangwei Zhou
- **Abstract**: Transformer models have demonstrated exceptional performance and have become indispensable in computer vision (CV) and natural language processing (NLP) tasks. However, recent studies reveal that transformers are susceptible to backdoor attacks. Prior backdoor attack methods typically rely on retraining with clean data or altering the model architecture, both of which can be resource-intensive and intrusive. In this paper, we propose Head-wise Pruning and Malicious Injection (HPMI), a novel retraining-free backdoor attack on transformers that does not alter the model's architecture. Our approach requires only a small subset of the original data and basic knowledge of the model architecture, eliminating the need for retraining the target transformer. Technically, HPMI works by pruning the least important head and injecting a pre-trained malicious head to establish the backdoor. We provide a rigorous theoretical justification demonstrating that the implanted backdoor resists detection and removal by state-of-the-art defense techniques, under reasonable assumptions. Experimental evaluations across multiple datasets further validate the effectiveness of HPMI, showing that it 1) incurs negligible clean accuracy loss, 2) achieves at least 99.55% attack success rate, and 3) bypasses four advanced defense mechanisms. Additionally, relative to state-of-the-art retraining-dependent attacks, HPMI achieves greater concealment and robustness against diverse defense strategies, while maintaining minimal impact on clean accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Pruning and Malicious Injection: A Retraining-Free Backdoor Attack on Transformer Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Head-wise Pruning and Malicious Injection (HPMI), a novel, retraining-free backdoor attack on transformer models. HPMI works by pruning the least important attention head in the model and injecting a pre-trained, single-head malicious transformer into the vacated position. The malicious head is trained to activate in the presence of a backdoor trigger, causing the model to misclassify inputs to a target class.  The paper provides a theoretical justification for HPMI's resilience against detection and removal, and validates its effectiveness through experiments across various datasets and models, including demonstrating its ability to bypass several defense mechanisms. The authors claim their attack maintains clean accuracy and achieves high attack success rates while being stealthier and more robust than retraining-dependent backdoor attacks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of a retraining-free backdoor attack is interesting, as it addresses the resource-intensive nature of existing attacks. Pruning followed by injection *in itself* isn't completely novel (pruning for backdoor defense is a known concept), but the way HPMI combines pruning the least important head with injecting a malicious head trained separately and the theoretical justification constitutes a level of novelty. The specific methodology tailored to transformer architectures is a contribution. However, the triggers used (noise patches, blended images, rare words) are relatively standard backdoor triggers.

*   **Significance:** The significance hinges on the practical implications of a retraining-free attack.
