If the attack truly bypasses strong defenses with minimal impact on clean accuracy, it would highlight a critical vulnerability in pre-trained transformer models. The paper's claims about stealthiness and robustness are crucial for establishing its significance. The demonstrated capacity to bypass defense mechanisms is significant. The data-free aspect (minimal original data needed) also increases practical relevance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Retraining-Free:** A significant advantage over existing attacks, making it more practical in real-world scenarios where access to the full training data is limited or non-existent.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The inclusion of a theoretical analysis is a strong point, providing some assurance of the attack's robustness against detection.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The extensive experimental evaluation across multiple datasets and models strengthens the paper's claims. Testing against multiple defense mechanisms also builds confidence in the attack's resilience.
    *   **Maintained Accuracy:** The claims of minimal impact on clean accuracy are well supported in the experiment section.
    *   **Attack Success Rate:** High attack success rates across different settings prove the attack is quite efficient.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Trigger Simplicity:** The reliance on common triggers (noise patches, blending, rare words) could be a limitation. More sophisticated, "semantic" triggers might be harder to detect. A demonstration using a more complex trigger would enhance the paper's credibility.
    *   **Practical Assumptions:** The assumption of full internal access to model parameters is a strong one. While it's a plausible supply-chain attack scenario, exploring variations where access is more limited (e.g., only to specific layers) would broaden the attack's applicability.
    *   **Limited Comparison:** While several defenses are tested, a more detailed comparison to other recent transformer backdoor attacks, especially in terms of stealth, is needed.
    *   **Definition of Important Head:** The methodology to determine the least important head by iterative evaluation seems inefficient and costly.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the results are validated by further research, this work could influence future defense strategies for transformer models. The concept of pruning and injecting specialized heads might inspire new architectural designs that are more robust against backdoors. It also serves as a warning to users of pre-trained models about the potential risks of malicious injections.

*   **Score Rationale:** The paper presents a novel approach with promising results, but some limitations weaken its overall impact. The attack's dependence on known triggers and the strong assumption of full parameter access, although reasonable in some scenarios, constrain its practical scope. More analysis of the cost and difficulty of selecting the "least important head" would add value. However, the demonstrated capacity to bypass defense mechanisms, maintain clean accuracy, and the retraining-free nature are significant strengths.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Meta-Metrics and Best Practices for System-Level Inference Performance Benchmarking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10251v1)
- **Authors**: Shweta Salaria, Zhuoran Liu, Nelson Mimura Gonzalez
- **Abstract**: Benchmarking inference performance (speed) of Foundation Models such as Large Language Models (LLM) involves navigating a vast experimental landscape to understand the complex interactions between hardware and software components. However, evaluating every possible test configuration is impractical, unfeasible and unnecessary. To address this challenge, we introduce FMwork, a comprehensive and methodical approach to creating a controlled testing environment that accurately reflects and characterizes performance. FMwork comprises a set of benchmkaring best practices with three key components: 1) meta-metrics, 2) parameter selection, and 3) strategic cost-performance evaluation. Meta-metrics account for time and resources spent on benchmarking and the relative accuracy of the results compared to a larger body of measurements, representing the complete experimental space. FMwork operationalizes the meta-metrics and provides efficient strategies for parameter selection and cost-performance analysis. Using the framework, we show up to 24x improvement (speedup and/or resource savings) running sweeps of experiments compared to the ground truth. Even already considering a subset of experiments as reference point (using the power of two for batch sizes), reducing experimental output size from 1024 to 128 tokens yields another 2.7x gain while keeping 96.6% accuracy for an evaluation using Llama 3.1 8B model.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FMwork, a comprehensive benchmarking framework and methodology for large language model (LLM) inference. It addresses the challenge of efficiently navigating the vast experimental space involved in benchmarking LLMs by proposing a set of best practices built around three key components: meta-metrics, parameter selection strategies, and cost-performance evaluation. The meta-metrics are designed to quantify the time, cost, and resources spent on benchmarking, alongside the accuracy of the results compared to a complete sample space. The parameter selection strategies aim to optimize sampling efficiency and cost-performance analysis.  Finally, it includes synthetic input and model generation for flexible evaluation.  The authors demonstrate significant speedups (up to 24x) and resource savings using FMwork, while maintaining acceptable accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a Practical Problem:** LLM benchmarking is computationally expensive and time-consuming. The paper directly tackles this challenge, providing a much-needed framework for efficient and reliable evaluation.
    *   **Comprehensive Methodology:** FMwork's multi-faceted approach, incorporating meta-metrics, parameter selection, and cost-performance analysis, is well-reasoned and provides a structured way to approach benchmarking.
    *   **Demonstrated Speedups:** The experimental results showing significant speedups (up to 24x) are compelling evidence of FMwork's effectiveness. The analyses considering power of two of batch sizes and decreasing sequence length show tangible improvement.
    *   **Open-Source Availability:** Making FMwork open-source promotes reproducibility and allows the community to build upon and improve the framework.
    *   **Synthetic Input and Model Generation:** This offers a flexible and controlled way to evaluate different workload scenarios and model architectures, which is crucial considering the rapid evolution of the field.
    *   **Meta-metric definition is novel:** The explicit consideration of time, cost and accuracy for benchmarks as first-class metrics is a novel view for the area and it is useful for improving benchmark efficiency.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Models and Hardware:** The experimental evaluation primarily focuses on a specific model (Llama 3.1-8b) and hardware (NVIDIA H100). While this provides concrete results, it limits the generalizability of the findings. Expanding the evaluation to include a wider range of models (e.g., MoE models, larger models) and hardware (e.g., different GPUs, TPUs) would strengthen the paper's conclusions.
    *   **Interpolation Accuracy:** While the approach addresses a huge search space, interpolation on all parameters, including TTFT and ITL, might introduce inaccuracies. Linear interpolation, while computationally simple, might not accurately capture the non-linear relationships between parameters.
    *   **Limited Comparison to Existing Benchmarking Tools:** While the related work section mentions other benchmarking tools, a more in-depth comparison of FMwork's advantages and disadvantages compared to those tools (e.g., LLM-Inference-Bench, MLPerf Inference) would be valuable. A table summarizing the key differences in methodology, scope, and functionality would improve clarity.
    *   **Synthetic Data Accuracy:** While the experiments show minimal difference between synthetic and real inputs, the approach focuses on aggregated throughput. Other factors are not evaluated, such as evaluation of bias/fairness on generated models or data.
    *   **Opaque Interpolation/Extrapolation Method:** The "interpolation/extrapolation" is not made fully explicit. Specifically, there is no explanation of *which* values for ITT and TTFT are interpolated, and there are no graphs showing those values before interpolation/extrapolation. This limits the possibility for full reproducibility.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

The paper introduces a well-structured and practically useful framework for optimizing LLM benchmarking. The novel contribution lies in the systematic approach to reducing the experimental space and managing the trade-offs between cost and accuracy. The demonstrated speedups and open-source availability make FMwork a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners in the field. The meta-metric framework provides an interesting lens to look at benchmarking runs for optimizing performance and efficiency.

*   **Potential Impact:**

FMwork has the potential to significantly impact the way LLM inference performance is evaluated. By providing a more efficient and reliable methodology, it can enable faster iteration cycles for model development and deployment, ultimately contributing to the advancement of the field. The synthetic model/data generation is a useful addition to allow a broader array of evaluation.

*Score: 7*

**Justification:**

A score of 7 is assigned because the paper addresses a relevant and pressing problem in the field of LLM inference benchmarking. FMwork's methodological contributions, particularly the meta-metrics and parameter selection strategies, are novel and well-defined. The reported speedups are significant and support the effectiveness of the framework.  The practical utility of the approach is enhanced by the open-source release.

However, the score is not higher due to the limited scope of the experimental evaluation and lack of a deeper comparative analysis with existing benchmarking tools. While the demonstrated benefits are promising, further validation across a wider range of models, hardware, and workload scenarios is needed to establish the generalizability of the findings. Additionally, full reproducibility is not explicit because of the lack of clarity around interpolation methods. Finally, bias and fairness evaluations are not included in the approach. These limitations prevent a higher assessment of novelty and significance.

- **Classification**: cs.PF
- **Score**: 7/10

### MRFD: Multi-Region Fusion Decoding with Self-Consistency for Mitigating Hallucinations in LVLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10264v1)
- **Authors**: Haonan Ge, Yiwei Wang, Ming-Hsuan Yang, Yujun Cai
- **Abstract**: Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) have shown strong performance across multimodal tasks. However, they often produce hallucinations -- text that is inconsistent with visual input, due to the limited ability to verify information in different regions of the image. To address this, we propose Multi-Region Fusion Decoding (MRFD), a training-free decoding method that improves factual grounding by modeling inter-region consistency. MRFD identifies salient regions using cross-attention, generates initial responses for each, and computes reliability weights based on Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) among the responses. These weights guide a consistency-aware fusion of per-region predictions, using region-aware prompts inspired by Chain-of-Thought reasoning. Experiments across multiple LVLMs and benchmarks show that MRFD significantly reduces hallucinations and improves response factuality without requiring model updates.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Multi-Region Fusion Decoding (MRFD), a novel training-free decoding method designed to mitigate hallucinations in Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs). The approach operates by identifying salient regions within an image using cross-attention mechanisms, generating initial responses for each region, and then computing reliability weights based on the Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) among these regional responses. These weights guide a consistency-aware fusion of the per-region predictions. Region-aware prompts, inspired by Chain-of-Thought reasoning, further enhance the process. Experiments across several LVLMs and benchmarks demonstrate that MRFD significantly reduces hallucinations and improves response factuality without requiring model updates.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty of the paper lies in its region-aware decoding strategy and the explicit use of inter-region consistency to mitigate hallucinations. While attention mechanisms and Chain-of-Thought prompting are established techniques, the specific combination of these with JSD-based weighting for region-level confidence and fusion is innovative. The concept of leveraging consistency *between visual regions* rather than just *between inference steps* is a valuable contribution. Prior work often treats images holistically or analyzes regions in isolation.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work is substantial. Hallucinations in LVLMs are a major impediment to their reliable deployment in real-world applications. Reducing these hallucinations without requiring retraining or fine-tuning opens up possibilities for using these models in more safety-critical or information-sensitive tasks. The training-free nature of the method makes it highly accessible and easily adaptable to different LVLMs. The consistent improvements shown across diverse models and benchmarks further highlight the practical value of the approach.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Training-free Approach:** Avoiding the need for retraining or fine-tuning is a major advantage, making the method practical and widely applicable.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly explains each step of the MRFD pipeline, including region selection, JSD-based weighting, and consistency-based fusion.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The experiments demonstrate consistent and significant improvements in reducing hallucinations across multiple LVLMs (LLaVA-1.5, InstructBLIP) and datasets (POPE, CHAIR, MME-Hallucination). The ablation studies further validate the importance of each component of the MRFD method.
    *   **Addresses an Important Problem:** Hallucinations are a significant barrier to the reliable deployment of LVLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on Attention Maps:** The method's effectiveness depends on the quality of attention maps produced by the underlying LVLM. If the attention mechanism is poor, the region selection will be suboptimal, potentially affecting the final performance.
    *   **Overhead of Multi-Region Analysis:** While the authors discuss the competitive efficiency profile, the multi-region analysis inherently increases the computational cost compared to standard decoding. The latency analysis reveals a 2.96x increase, which could be a limiting factor in some real-time applications.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The method is primarily evaluated on object hallucination tasks in images. It's unclear how well it would generalize to other types of hallucinations (e.g., attribute or relationship errors) or to other modalities (e.g., video or audio).  The limited scope of downstream tasks examined limits conclusions to the general usability of the method.
    *   **Parameter Sensitivity:** The performance depends on the appropriate setting for hyperparameters.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:
    *   It provides a strong baseline for training-free hallucination mitigation techniques.
    *   It highlights the importance of inter-region consistency as a signal for factual grounding.
    *   It encourages further exploration of region-aware decoding strategies in LVLMs.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to a significant problem in LVLMs. The thorough experimental evaluation and ablation studies strongly support the effectiveness of the proposed method. The training-free nature and broad applicability of MRFD make it a valuable contribution to the field. While the method has some limitations, such as its dependence on attention maps and increased computational cost, these are relatively minor compared to the overall value of the work. Therefore, the paper merits a strong score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Why Cannot Large Language Models Ever Make True Correct Reasoning?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10265v1)
- **Authors**: Jingde Cheng
- **Abstract**: Recently, with the application progress of AIGC tools based on large language models (LLMs), led by ChatGPT, many AI experts and more non-professionals are trumpeting the "understanding ability" and "reasoning ability" of the LLMs. The present author considers that the so-called "understanding ability" and "reasoning ability" of LLMs are just illusions of those people who with vague concepts. In fact, the LLMs can never have the true understanding ability and true reasoning ability. This paper intents to explain that, because the essential limitations of their working principle, the LLMs can never have the ability of true correct reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Why Cannot Large Language Models Ever Make True Correct Reasoning?"

**Summary:**

The paper argues that Large Language Models (LLMs), despite the hype surrounding their "understanding" and "reasoning" abilities, can never achieve true correct reasoning.  The author claims this is due to the fundamental limitations of their working principle, which relies on statistical distributions rather than a formal logic system. The paper defines "true correct reasoning" as requiring conclusive relevant evidence for conclusions, necessitating a built-in correctness evaluation criterion and dynamic evaluation mechanism, which LLMs inherently lack.  The author reviews logical systems, arguing that Strong Relevant Logics (SRLs) are necessary for proper reasoning and points out that LLMs cannot implement such systems within their current architecture. The author dismisses claims of LLM reasoning as illusions stemming from human misunderstanding of reasoning, copying of human reasoning examples, the ELIZA effect, and the ability to solve problems that some humans find difficult. Ultimately, the paper contends that LLMs can only simulate the *form* of reasoning, lacking the capacity for genuine correctness.  It concludes that pursuing LLM reasoning abilities without addressing correctness evaluation is a misguided effort.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper raises a valid and important question about the nature of reasoning in LLMs. It correctly highlights the distinction between generating text that *resembles* reasoning and performing actual, logically sound inference. The emphasis on correctness, validity, and relevance in reasoning is also critical. The author's focus on the need for a formal logic system to guarantee correctness provides a strong theoretical foundation for their argument. The paper also provides a helpful summary of the current state of affairs with LLMs and their supposed reasoning abilities, and touches upon valid reasons why people might mistake their capabilities for actual reasoning.

However, the paper suffers from several weaknesses that temper its overall impact:

*   **Oversimplification and Strawman Arguments:**  The paper often portrays LLM capabilities as mere "illusions" without fully engaging with the complexity of the field. While LLMs certainly have limitations, advancements in areas like neuro-symbolic AI and methods to improve reasoning reliability aren't sufficiently acknowledged.  The paper often attacks what is presented as LLMs "reasoning ability" as if it exists as a single, static thing, when there are many different approaches to improving reliability in the results LLMs output. There's a consistent undertone of dismissing the entire field.
*   **Rigidity in Defining Reasoning:** The definition of "true correct reasoning" as requiring 100% certainty is an extremely strict standard.  While valuable for certain critical applications, it doesn't necessarily invalidate the usefulness of LLMs in other contexts where probabilistic reasoning or approximate solutions are acceptable.  The author seemingly fails to acknowledge the existence of other types of reasoning that may not require "100% conclusive" relevant evidence.
*   **Limited Engagement with Counterarguments:** The paper mentions some research that praises LLM reasoning, but does not thoroughly engage with the mechanisms by which this is said to be achieved. The paper could benefit from a more balanced discussion, addressing potential avenues for improvement in LLM reasoning and discussing more recent developments in the field since the time the paper was written.
*   **Lack of Concrete Alternatives:** While the paper advocates for Strong Relevant Logics (SRLs), it doesn't provide concrete suggestions for how to integrate these logics into LLM architectures in a practical way. It remains theoretical, leaving a gap between the problem and the proposed solution.
*   **Outdated Literature Review:** The literature review, even accounting for the "just accepted in 2025" publication on LLMs, seems somewhat limited. Given the rapid pace of development in the LLM field, newer approaches to enhancing reasoning and addressing limitations may not have been adequately considered.

Despite these weaknesses, the paper has some merit in its critique of the uncritical acceptance of LLM reasoning. It serves as a valuable reminder to carefully evaluate the correctness and reliability of LLM outputs, rather than blindly accepting them as truth. Its emphasis on formal logic as a potential foundation for robust reasoning is also a worthwhile direction for future research.

**Score: 5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valid critique of LLM reasoning capabilities, highlighting the need for correctness evaluation. It correctly identifies the lack of a formal logical framework as a fundamental limitation. However, the oversimplified portrayal of LLMs, the rigid definition of reasoning, the limited engagement with counterarguments, and the lack of concrete solutions weaken the overall impact. The paper is also marred by its somewhat derisive tone. While the core argument has value, the shortcomings in execution warrant a moderate score. Its significance is limited by its lack of practical suggestions and its failure to fully acknowledge the ongoing evolution of the LLM field. A score of 5 reflects its moderately important, but imperfectly executed, contribution to the ongoing debate about LLM reasoning.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 5/10

### High Fidelity Text to Image Generation with Contrastive Alignment and Structural Guidance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10280v1)
- **Authors**: Danyi Gao
- **Abstract**: This paper addresses the performance bottlenecks of existing text-driven image generation methods in terms of semantic alignment accuracy and structural consistency. A high-fidelity image generation method is proposed by integrating text-image contrastive constraints with structural guidance mechanisms. The approach introduces a contrastive learning module that builds strong cross-modal alignment constraints to improve semantic matching between text and image. At the same time, structural priors such as semantic layout maps or edge sketches are used to guide the generator in spatial-level structural modeling. This enhances the layout completeness and detail fidelity of the generated images. Within the overall framework, the model jointly optimizes contrastive loss, structural consistency loss, and semantic preservation loss. A multi-objective supervision mechanism is adopted to improve the semantic consistency and controllability of the generated content. Systematic experiments are conducted on the COCO-2014 dataset. Sensitivity analyses are performed on embedding dimensions, text length, and structural guidance strength. Quantitative metrics confirm the superior performance of the proposed method in terms of CLIP Score, FID, and SSIM. The results show that the method effectively bridges the gap between semantic alignment and structural fidelity without increasing computational complexity. It demonstrates a strong ability to generate semantically clear and structurally complete images, offering a viable technical path for joint text-image modeling and image generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a high-fidelity text-to-image generation method that aims to improve semantic alignment and structural consistency, two common bottlenecks in existing text-to-image models. It achieves this by integrating text-image contrastive learning with structural guidance. The contrastive learning module enforces semantic matching between text and generated images by building strong cross-modal alignment constraints. Structural priors, like semantic layout maps or edge sketches, guide the generator at the spatial level, enhancing layout completeness and detail fidelity. The model is trained using a multi-objective loss function that combines contrastive loss, structural consistency loss, and semantic preservation loss.  Experiments on the COCO-2014 dataset demonstrate improved CLIP Score, FID, and SSIM metrics compared to baseline models, suggesting better semantic alignment and structural accuracy. The paper also explores the impact of embedding dimensions and text length on generation performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining contrastive learning with structural guidance isn't entirely new; both techniques have been used in text-to-image generation before. The novelty lies in the specific *integration* of these two components and the careful design of the loss functions to balance semantic and structural constraints. The multi-objective loss function and the analysis of hyperparameter settings (embedding dimensions, text length) represent incremental contributions. The paper's title implies a more groundbreaking innovation than what is present in the work. The authors present their idea as somewhat novel, citing existing research but not fully explaining the difference between their approach and those earlier contributions.

*   **Significance:**  Improving semantic alignment and structural consistency in text-to-image generation is a significant goal.  The paper demonstrates quantitative improvements on standard metrics, which suggests a practical benefit.  The paper suggests a path towards more controllable and semantically accurate image generation, which has broad applications in areas such as creative design, virtual reality, and medical image synthesis. However, the actual impact will depend on how well the method generalizes to other datasets and more complex scenarios, which the paper doesn't thoroughly address.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The integration of contrastive learning and structural guidance is well-motivated.
    *   The multi-objective loss function provides a mechanism for balancing different objectives.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate quantitative improvements over baseline models.
    *   The sensitivity analysis provides useful insights into the model's behavior.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The individual components (contrastive learning, structural guidance) are not novel in themselves. The innovation is in how they are combined, but this combination isn't radically different from prior work in the field.
    *   The quantitative improvements, while present, are not exceptionally large.
    *   The paper lacks a strong qualitative analysis.  Visual examples are needed to fully assess the quality of the generated images, and there are none present.
    *   The method's generalizability to other datasets and more complex scenarios is not adequately addressed. The evaluation is limited to the COCO-2014 dataset.
    *   The paper could benefit from a deeper discussion of the limitations of the approach. For instance, how does the method handle rare objects or complex relationships between objects? Does it struggle with specific types of scenes?

* **Impact on the field:** The paper offers a viable technical path for joint text-image modeling and image generation, so it can be useful for researchers in the field.

* **Overall:** The paper describes a solid piece of engineering that combines existing techniques in a sensible way to achieve incremental improvements. It does not represent a major breakthrough, but the results are promising and contribute to the ongoing effort of improving text-to-image generation. It's more of an advancement than a completely new invention.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:** The paper's score reflects its incremental contribution to the field. The novelty is limited, but the combination of contrastive learning and structural guidance is well-executed and produces quantitative improvements. The lack of qualitative examples and a more thorough analysis of limitations prevents a higher score. The paper's overall significance is moderate, primarily as a demonstration of the benefits of combining existing techniques.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### JRDB-Reasoning: A Difficulty-Graded Benchmark for Visual Reasoning in Robotics
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10287v1)
- **Authors**: Simindokht Jahangard, Mehrzad Mohammadi, Yi Shen, Zhixi Cai, Hamid Rezatofighi
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Vision-Language Models (VLMs) and large language models (LLMs) have greatly enhanced visual reasoning, a key capability for embodied AI agents like robots. However, existing visual reasoning benchmarks often suffer from several limitations: they lack a clear definition of reasoning complexity, offer have no control to generate questions over varying difficulty and task customization, and fail to provide structured, step-by-step reasoning annotations (workflows). To bridge these gaps, we formalize reasoning complexity, introduce an adaptive query engine that generates customizable questions of varying complexity with detailed intermediate annotations, and extend the JRDB dataset with human-object interaction and geometric relationship annotations to create JRDB-Reasoning, a benchmark tailored for visual reasoning in human-crowded environments. Our engine and benchmark enable fine-grained evaluation of visual reasoning frameworks and dynamic assessment of visual-language models across reasoning levels.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper. Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces JRDB-Reasoning, a new difficulty-graded benchmark for visual reasoning in robotics. Addressing limitations in existing benchmarks, the authors formalize reasoning complexity based on the number of entities, relationships, and temporal dynamics in a scene. They introduce an adaptive query engine that generates customizable questions of varying complexity and provides detailed intermediate reasoning annotations (workflows). The JRDB dataset is extended with human-object interaction and geometric relationship annotations to facilitate visual reasoning in human-crowded environments. The benchmark is designed for evaluating visual reasoning frameworks and dynamically assessing visual-language models (VLMs) across different reasoning levels.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its structured approach to defining and controlling the complexity of visual reasoning tasks. While existing benchmarks often present a mix of easy and hard examples, JRDB-Reasoning allows for explicit control over the difficulty level by manipulating the number of entities, relationships, and temporal slots. The adaptive query engine is also a valuable contribution, enabling the generation of diverse and customizable questions with intermediate annotations. Integrating HOI and geometric relations to the existing JRDB is another good addition.

*   **Significance:** Visual reasoning is a critical capability for embodied AI agents like robots. By providing a benchmark that allows for fine-grained evaluation of reasoning capabilities, JRDB-Reasoning has the potential to significantly advance research in this area. The adaptive query engine and detailed annotations can facilitate the development of more robust and explainable visual reasoning models. The ability to benchmark VLMs across reasoning levels is also important.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Definition of Reasoning Complexity:** The formalization of reasoning complexity based on entities, relationships, and temporal dynamics provides a useful framework for understanding and controlling task difficulty.
    *   **Adaptive Query Engine:** The engine generates customizable questions with detailed reasoning annotations, enabling fine-grained evaluation of model capabilities.
    *   **Integration of HOI and Geometric Relationships:** The expanded dataset provides richer contextual information for visual reasoning tasks.
    *   **Difficulty-Graded Benchmark:** Allows for controlled evaluation of models across various reasoning levels.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Evaluation:** The paper only presents a relatively small set of experiments evaluating a few existing VLMs. A more comprehensive evaluation with a wider range of models and tasks would strengthen the paper's claims.
    *   **Dependency on Existing Dataset:** The paper relies on the existing JRDB dataset, which may have its own limitations and biases. While the extensions are beneficial, the benchmark's overall quality depends on the underlying data.
    *   **Focus on Simpler Subset:** The paper mentions a focus on simpler question sets in current model capabilities which can mean that the engine itself is much better than the models that it currently tests.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    *   JRDB-Reasoning has the potential to become a valuable resource for researchers working on visual reasoning in robotics and embodied AI.
    *   The adaptive query engine and detailed annotations can facilitate the development of more robust and explainable visual reasoning models.
    *   The benchmark can be used to track progress in visual reasoning capabilities over time.

*   **Justification for Score:**
    The paper presents a solid contribution by introducing a novel difficulty-graded benchmark for visual reasoning. The structured approach to defining reasoning complexity, the adaptive query engine, and detailed annotations are valuable assets that address existing limitations in the field. However, the paper is somewhat limited by the scope of model evaluation and reliance on an existing dataset. For these reasons, the paper warrants a score that reflects its merits while acknowledging its limitations. The score is therefore set to an 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### DiffAxE: Diffusion-driven Hardware Accelerator Generation and Design Space Exploration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10303v1)
- **Authors**: Arkapravo Ghosh, Abhishek Moitra, Abhiroop Bhattacharjee, Ruokai Yin, Priyadarshini Panda
- **Abstract**: Design space exploration (DSE) is critical for developing optimized hardware architectures, especially for AI workloads such as deep neural networks (DNNs) and large language models (LLMs), which require specialized acceleration. As model complexity grows, accelerator design spaces have expanded to O(10^17), becoming highly irregular, non-convex, and exhibiting many-to-one mappings from design configurations to performance metrics. This complexity renders direct inverse derivation infeasible and necessitates heuristic or sampling-based optimization. Conventional methods - including Bayesian optimization, gradient descent, reinforcement learning, and genetic algorithms - depend on iterative sampling, resulting in long runtimes and sensitivity to initialization. Deep learning-based approaches have reframed DSE as classification using recommendation models, but remain limited to small-scale (O(10^3)), less complex design spaces. To overcome these constraints, we propose a generative approach that models hardware design as 1-D image synthesis conditioned on target performance, enabling efficient learning of non-differentiable, non-bijective hardware-performance mappings. Our framework achieves 0.86% lower generation error than Bayesian optimization with a 17000x speedup, and outperforms GANDSE with 30% lower error at only 1.83x slower search. We further extend the method to a structured DSE setting, attaining 9.8% lower energy-delay product (EDP) and 6% higher performance, with up to 145.6x and 1312x faster search compared to existing optimization methods on O(10^17) design spaces. For LLM inference, our method achieves 3.37x and 7.75x lower EDP on a 32nm ASIC and Xilinx Ultrascale+ VPU13 FPGA, respectively, compared to the state-of-the-art DOSA framework.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DiffAxE, a novel generative framework for automating the design and exploration of hardware accelerators for AI workloads. DiffAxE utilizes a denoising diffusion probabilistic model (DDPM) to learn the complex, non-differentiable mapping between hardware configurations, workloads, and performance metrics.  It encodes hardware configurations into a latent space, conditioned on target performance and workload, enabling efficient generation of hardware designs that meet specific performance constraints. The framework is shown to achieve significant speedups compared to traditional DSE methods like Bayesian optimization and gradient descent while maintaining high accuracy. DiffAxE is further extended to EDP optimization, allowing the discovery of energy-efficient hardware designs. The paper validates DiffAxE on a range of DNN workloads and compares its performance against several baselines, demonstrating its effectiveness in both ASIC and FPGA implementations, especially for LLM inference.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of diffusion models to hardware accelerator design is relatively novel. While generative models, including GANs, have been explored in this space (GANDSE), the use of DDPMs offers advantages in terms of training stability and the ability to capture multimodal distributions in the design space, addressing the many-to-one mapping challenge in hardware design. The performance-guided latent space encoding is a significant improvement. Previous approaches often fall short in handling the complexities and irregularities of the hardware-performance landscape.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of DiffAxE on the field of AI accelerator design is significant. The ability to rapidly generate optimized hardware designs can dramatically reduce the time and effort required to develop application-specific accelerators. The extension to EDP optimization further enhances its practicality, enabling the creation of energy-efficient hardware. The results on LLM inference, a crucial area of AI, show substantial improvements over existing methods.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Scalability:** DiffAxE demonstrates scalability to massive design spaces (O(10^17)), addressing a key limitation of previous approaches.
    *   **Speed and Accuracy:** Achieves significant speedups (17322x over BO) while maintaining accuracy in performance prediction, mitigating the limitations of traditional heuristic-driven exploration.
    *   **Generalization:** The framework exhibits strong generalization capabilities across a diverse set of DNN workloads.
    *   **EDP Optimization:** Successfully integrates EDP optimization, enabling the discovery of energy-efficient hardware designs.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The paper provides thorough experimental validation, comparing DiffAxE against a variety of strong baselines on both ASIC and FPGA platforms.
    *   **Complete methodology:** Includes implementation specifics, ensuring reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Simulation:** The training of DiffAxE still depends on a cycle-accurate simulator (Scale-Sim) to generate performance data.  While DiffAxE accelerates the DSE process, the simulation cost remains a factor. Future work could explore ways to reduce this dependency, perhaps using surrogate models or transfer learning to improve data efficiency.
    *   **Hardware Cost:** All the hardware configurations are explored for the same amount of time/number of cycles by Scale-Sim. However, the hardware cost or compute resources required may not be similar for all designs, and some designs may be easier to achieve, while others are computationally prohibitive.
    *   **FPGA Results:** The FPGA implementation results seem to be constrained to a single BERT baseline architecture. More experiments may be required to generalize the usefulness of the methodology.

*   **Justification for Score:** The core contribution of DiffAxE – a generative framework utilizing diffusion models for rapid and accurate hardware accelerator design space exploration – represents a significant advancement. It directly addresses limitations in existing DSE methods and shows tangible improvements across various metrics. While the reliance on simulators is a constraint, DiffAxE significantly mitigates their impact by dramatically accelerating the overall DSE process. The LLM inference results highlight the potential of DiffAxE to impact a crucial and rapidly evolving area of AI. However, some of the experiments are limited, and the dependence on simulations is significant.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Yet another algorithmic bias: A Discursive Analysis of Large Language Models Reinforcing Dominant Discourses on Gender and Race
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10304v1)
- **Authors**: Gustavo Bonil, Simone Hashiguti, Jhessica Silva, João Gondim, Helena Maia, Nádia Silva, Helio Pedrini, Sandra Avila
- **Abstract**: With the advance of Artificial Intelligence (AI), Large Language Models (LLMs) have gained prominence and been applied in diverse contexts. As they evolve into more sophisticated versions, it is essential to assess whether they reproduce biases, such as discrimination and racialization, while maintaining hegemonic discourses. Current bias detection approaches rely mostly on quantitative, automated methods, which often overlook the nuanced ways in which biases emerge in natural language. This study proposes a qualitative, discursive framework to complement such methods. Through manual analysis of LLM-generated short stories featuring Black and white women, we investigate gender and racial biases. We contend that qualitative methods such as the one proposed here are fundamental to help both developers and users identify the precise ways in which biases manifest in LLM outputs, thus enabling better conditions to mitigate them. Results show that Black women are portrayed as tied to ancestry and resistance, while white women appear in self-discovery processes. These patterns reflect how language models replicate crystalized discursive representations, reinforcing essentialization and a sense of social immobility. When prompted to correct biases, models offered superficial revisions that maintained problematic meanings, revealing limitations in fostering inclusive narratives. Our results demonstrate the ideological functioning of algorithms and have significant implications for the ethical use and development of AI. The study reinforces the need for critical, interdisciplinary approaches to AI design and deployment, addressing how LLM-generated discourses reflect and perpetuate inequalities.
- **Summary**: This paper investigates algorithmic bias in Large Language Models (LLMs) concerning gender and race, focusing on narratives generated about Black and white women. The authors use a qualitative, discursive framework to analyze texts generated by several LLMs, finding that the models reinforce stereotypical narratives: Black women are depicted as tied to ancestry and resistance, while white women are portrayed in self-discovery processes. The study also shows that when prompted to correct biases, the LLMs offer superficial revisions that maintain problematic meanings. The authors argue for the importance of qualitative methods to complement quantitative approaches in identifying and mitigating biases in LLMs. Their analysis highlights the ideological functioning of algorithms and its implications for the ethical use and development of AI.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its methodological approach. While many studies examine bias in LLMs, few employ a qualitative, discursive analysis grounded in decolonial thought and Critical Applied Linguistics. This approach provides a nuanced understanding of how biases are expressed through language, going beyond simple quantitative measurements. The focus on Portuguese-language models is also a valuable contribution, as this area is often underrepresented.

**Significance:** The paper's significance lies in several aspects. First, it underscores the limitations of relying solely on quantitative methods for bias detection in LLMs, demonstrating the importance of human interpretation and contextual understanding. Second, it provides a concrete example of how LLMs can perpetuate harmful stereotypes about race and gender through subtle linguistic choices. Third, it highlights the difficulty LLMs have in identifying and correcting these biases, even when explicitly prompted. This is important for developers and users to know. Fourth, it contributes to the growing body of research on algorithmic racism and its implications for social justice. Finally, the explicit positioning within Critical Applied Linguistics offers a strong political grounding for the research.

**Strengths:**

*   **Strong theoretical framework:** The paper effectively integrates discursive analysis, decolonial thought, and Critical Applied Linguistics to provide a robust and insightful framework for analyzing bias in LLMs.
*   **Nuanced analysis:** The qualitative approach allows for a deep and nuanced understanding of how biases are expressed in the generated texts, going beyond simple quantitative measurements.
*   **Clear methodology:** The authors clearly describe their methodology, including the construction of the corpus, the analytical framework, and the justification for their interpretive approach.
*   **Focus on Portuguese-language models:** The inclusion of Portuguese-language models adds a valuable perspective to the existing literature, which is often dominated by English-language models.
*   **Critically positioned research:** The authors are very clear on their biases and what guides their research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited generalizability:** The qualitative nature of the analysis and the relatively small corpus size limit the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Focus on specific models:** The analysis is based on specific versions of LLMs, which may evolve over time. Results can change across versions.
*   **Lack of technical solutions:** The paper focuses on identifying and analyzing bias but does not offer concrete technical solutions for mitigating it. This is a limitation given the title and the need for this kind of work in the field of Computer Science.
*   **Repetitive findings:** Although the analysis is rigorous, the consistent reinforcement of known biases may not strike all readers as highly novel.

**Justification:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by offering a nuanced qualitative perspective on bias in LLMs, particularly concerning the intersection of race and gender, and by centering its analysis in a less-studied linguistic context. While limited in generalizability and providing technical solutions, it effectively highlights the limitations of current approaches to bias detection and the importance of human interpretation. The study's strength in utilizing a robust theoretical framework warrants a higher-than-average score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Beyond Semantic Understanding: Preserving Collaborative Frequency Components in LLM-based Recommendation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10312v1)
- **Authors**: Minhao Wang, Yunhang He, Cong Xu, Zhangchi Zhu, Wei Zhang
- **Abstract**: Recommender systems in concert with Large Language Models (LLMs) present promising avenues for generating semantically-informed recommendations. However, LLM-based recommenders exhibit a tendency to overemphasize semantic correlations within users' interaction history. When taking pretrained collaborative ID embeddings as input, LLM-based recommenders progressively weaken the inherent collaborative signals as the embeddings propagate through LLM backbones layer by layer, as opposed to traditional Transformer-based sequential models in which collaborative signals are typically preserved or even enhanced for state-of-the-art performance. To address this limitation, we introduce FreLLM4Rec, an approach designed to balance semantic and collaborative information from a spectral perspective. Item embeddings that incorporate both semantic and collaborative information are first purified using a Global Graph Low-Pass Filter (G-LPF) to preliminarily remove irrelevant high-frequency noise. Temporal Frequency Modulation (TFM) then actively preserves collaborative signal layer by layer. Note that the collaborative preservation capability of TFM is theoretically guaranteed by establishing a connection between the optimal but hard-to-implement local graph fourier filters and the suboptimal yet computationally efficient frequency-domain filters. Extensive experiments on four benchmark datasets demonstrate that FreLLM4Rec successfully mitigates collaborative signal attenuation and achieves competitive performance, with improvements of up to 8.00\% in NDCG@10 over the best baseline. Our findings provide insights into how LLMs process collaborative information and offer a principled approach for improving LLM-based recommendation systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses a key limitation of using Large Language Models (LLMs) for recommendation systems: the "Intra-Layer Spectral Attenuation" phenomenon.  It argues that LLMs, when used with pre-trained collaborative ID embeddings, progressively weaken inherent collaborative signals as the embeddings propagate through the LLM's layers. This is in contrast to traditional Transformer-based sequential models where such signals are often preserved or enhanced.  To counteract this, the authors propose FreLLM4Rec, a framework designed to balance semantic understanding and collaborative information from a spectral perspective. FreLLM4Rec uses a Global Graph Low-Pass Filter (G-LPF) to initially remove high-frequency noise from item embeddings and then employs Temporal Frequency Modulation (TFM) to actively preserve collaborative signals layer by layer. The authors provide theoretical justification connecting their frequency-domain filtering to equivalent graph-based approaches. Experiments on several datasets demonstrate FreLLM4Rec's ability to mitigate signal attenuation and achieve competitive recommendation performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The identification of "Intra-Layer Spectral Attenuation" is a significant contribution.  While the notion that LLMs can struggle with collaborative information has been hinted at previously, the authors provide a clear, spectral-domain explanation for this phenomenon.  The connection to graph signal processing is a valuable tool for understanding the LLM's internal dynamics.  The proposed FreLLM4Rec framework, while drawing upon existing frequency-domain techniques (Butterworth filters, DFT), is novel in its application to this specific problem within LLM-based recommendation.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to improve the effectiveness of LLM-based recommendation systems.  By addressing a fundamental issue in how LLMs process collaborative information, FreLLM4Rec offers a principled approach to leverage the semantic power of LLMs without sacrificing crucial collaborative filtering signals. The reported improvements in NDCG@10 demonstrate tangible benefits. The approach is particularly impactful because it can easily be integrated into existing LLM-based recommendation pipelines. The work sheds light on the importance of understanding the internal workings of LLMs in recommendation, moving beyond treating them as black boxes.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The "Intra-Layer Spectral Attenuation" phenomenon is clearly defined and well-motivated.
    *   **Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides a strong theoretical basis for the proposed solution, connecting temporal frequency modulation to graph spectral properties.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments are extensive, using multiple benchmark datasets and comparing against a variety of strong baselines.  Ablation studies clearly demonstrate the contribution of each component of FreLLM4Rec.
    *   **Robustness Analysis:** Demonstrating the approach's effectiveness across different LLM architectures and embedding sources adds to the paper's credibility.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Assumption 1 (Spatio-Temporal Locality):** The core theoretical argument relies on the assumption of spatio-temporal locality. While this is a common assumption in recommendation, its validity may vary depending on the dataset and application.  A more detailed discussion of the limitations and potential impact of this assumption would be beneficial.
    *   **Complexity of Implementation:** While the authors claim the approach maintains the scalability of standard LLM architectures, the addition of G-LPF and TFM modules does introduce additional computational overhead. A more detailed analysis of the practical computational costs would be helpful.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has a strong potential to influence the field of LLM-based recommendation. The identified problem is relevant, the proposed solution is well-justified, and the experimental results are convincing.  It is likely to spur further research into understanding and mitigating spectral attenuation in LLMs and other neural architectures for recommendation, as well as promoting further exploration of frequency-aware techniques. The paper also provides a valuable framework for analyzing and interpreting the internal dynamics of LLMs when applied to recommendation tasks.
Score: 7.  While the problem identified and the solution presented are impactful and theoretically sound, the validity hinges on assumptions regarding spatio-temporal locality, and there is a need to examine the impact of G-LPF and TFM on computational costs.


- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Cross-Prompt Encoder for Low-Performing Languages
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10352v1)
- **Authors**: Beso Mikaberidze, Teimuraz Saghinadze, Simon Ostermann, Philipp Muller
- **Abstract**: Soft prompts have emerged as a powerful alternative to adapters in parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT), enabling large language models (LLMs) to adapt to downstream tasks without architectural changes or parameter updates. While prior work has focused on stabilizing training via parameter interaction in small neural prompt encoders, their broader potential for transfer across languages remains unexplored. In this paper, we demonstrate that a prompt encoder can play a central role in improving performance on low-performing languages-those that achieve poor accuracy even under full-model fine-tuning. We introduce the Cross-Prompt Encoder (XPE), which combines a lightweight encoding architecture with multi-source training on typologically diverse languages - a design that enables the model to capture abstract and transferable patterns across languages. To complement XPE, we propose a Dual Soft Prompt mechanism that combines an encoder-based prompt with a directly trained standard soft prompt. This hybrid design proves especially effective for target languages that benefit from both broadly shared structure and language-specific alignment. Experiments on the SIB-200 benchmark reveal a consistent trade-off: XPE is most effective for low-performing languages, while hybrid variants offer broader adaptability across multilingual settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Cross-Prompt Encoder (XPE) for improving the performance of large language models (LLMs) on low-performing languages in cross-lingual task transfer. XPE utilizes a small, reusable neural prompt encoder trained on multiple typologically diverse languages to learn abstract, language-agnostic patterns.  The paper also proposes a Dual Soft Prompt (DUAL) mechanism that combines XPE with a standard soft prompt (SPT) to incorporate both abstract patterns and language-specific cues. Experiments on the SIB-200 benchmark demonstrate that XPE is effective for low-performing languages, while DUAL offers broader adaptability across multilingual settings. The authors ablate the components and compare their approaches with several baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The idea of using a prompt encoder for cross-lingual transfer isn't entirely novel, as the paper acknowledges related work like P-tuning and MPT. The specific architecture of the XPE, with its focus on multi-source training and static encoding at inference, is somewhat novel.  The DUAL prompt mechanism is a sensible extension that addresses the trade-off between generalizability and specialization. However, the incremental nature of these improvements limits the degree of novelty.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem: improving the performance of LLMs on low-resource or low-performing languages. This is a crucial step toward more equitable access to NLP technologies. Demonstrating improvements on the SIB-200 benchmark, with its large number of diverse languages, adds credibility to the approach. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of each component. The performance improvements on low-performing languages, which often lag behind in multilingual benchmarks, are practically valuable. The parameter-efficient nature of the proposed methods is also a valuable contribution, enabling wider deployment.

* **Strengths:**
    * Clear problem statement and motivation.
    * Thorough experimental evaluation on a challenging benchmark (SIB-200).
    * Detailed ablation studies that provide insights into the contribution of each component.
    *  Comparison against several strong baselines, including zero-shot LLMs and full-model fine-tuning.
    *  Emphasis on improving performance on low-performing languages.
    *  Parameter-efficient approach.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The approach builds upon existing prompt tuning methods, and the architectural modifications are relatively incremental.
    * While the results are promising, the performance gains over some baselines aren't always substantial, particularly on non-low-performing languages.
    * The analysis, while detailed, relies heavily on the SIB-200 dataset. Generalizability to other tasks and datasets could be strengthened.
    * The paper doesn't delve deeply into *why* the XPE and DUAL configurations work as they do. A deeper qualitative analysis or investigation of the learned representations could strengthen the work.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on cross-lingual transfer learning, particularly in the context of low-resource languages. The XPE and DUAL mechanisms offer practical strategies for improving performance with minimal parameter updates. The analysis of the trade-offs between generalizability and specialization will be valuable to other researchers in the field.

**Justification for the score:**

While the paper makes a solid contribution to the field of cross-lingual transfer learning, the novelty is limited by its reliance on existing prompt tuning techniques.  The significance is clear due to the focus on low-performing languages and the demonstration of improvements on a large, diverse benchmark. However, the results don't represent a paradigm shift, but rather an incremental improvement on existing methods.  The thoroughness of the experiments and analysis strengthens the paper, but the lack of deeper qualitative analysis and the reliance on a single dataset somewhat limit its impact. Therefore, while a valuable and technically sound piece of work, I give a score that reflects incremental but practically important contributions.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Making Qwen3 Think in Korean with Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10355v1)
- **Authors**: Jungyup Lee, Jemin Kim, Sang Park, SeungJae Lee
- **Abstract**: We present a two-stage fine-tuning approach to make the large language model Qwen3 14B "think" natively in Korean. In the first stage, supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on a high-quality Korean reasoning dataset establishes a strong foundation in Korean logical reasoning, yielding notable improvements in Korean-language tasks and even some gains in general reasoning ability. In the second stage, we employ reinforcement learning with a customized Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) algorithm to further enhance both Korean reasoning alignment and overall problem-solving performance. We address critical stability challenges in GRPO training - such as reward hacking and policy collapse - by introducing an oracle judge model that calibrates the reward signal. Our approach achieves stable learning (avoiding the collapse observed in naive GRPO) and leads to steady, incremental performance gains. The final RL-tuned model demonstrates substantially improved results on advanced reasoning benchmarks (particularly math and coding tasks) while maintaining knowledge and language proficiency, successfully conducting its internal chain-of-thought entirely in Korean.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a two-stage fine-tuning approach to enhance the Korean reasoning capabilities of the Qwen3 14B large language model. The first stage involves supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on a curated Korean reasoning dataset to establish a strong foundation in Korean language understanding and logical reasoning. The second stage employs reinforcement learning (RL) with a customized Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) algorithm, further enhanced by an oracle judge model, to refine both Korean reasoning alignment and overall problem-solving performance. The authors address stability challenges in GRPO training, such as reward hacking and policy collapse, through the oracle judge calibration.  The resulting model demonstrates improved performance on advanced reasoning benchmarks while maintaining knowledge and language proficiency, and performs its internal chain-of-thought reasoning natively in Korean.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates a practical and well-engineered approach to adapt an existing LLM to think natively in a non-English language. The two-stage fine-tuning, combining SFT and RL with modifications to GRPO, addresses a specific limitation of existing models. While the individual components (SFT, GRPO) are not novel in isolation, their combination, especially the introduction of the oracle judge within the GRPO reward loop to stabilize training, represents a non-trivial engineering contribution. The specific application to the Korean language and the demonstrated shift in the model's internal reasoning process also add to the novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles an important problem - the English language bias inherent in many LLMs. By demonstrating how to shift the internal reasoning process to another language, it opens the door for more culturally relevant and contextually accurate AI applications for non-English speaking users. This has significant implications for accessibility and inclusivity in AI.  The methods to stabilize RL fine-tuning for reasoning, particularly the Oracle-Guided Dr. GRPO technique, are valuable for the broader research community working on aligning LLMs for complex tasks. The performance improvements on reasoning benchmarks also showcase the practical effectiveness of the approach.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined methodology with a two-stage fine-tuning process.
    *   Address critical instability issues in RL fine-tuning with novel oracle-guided approach.
    *   Empirical evidence demonstrating performance improvements on Korean reasoning.
    *   Qualitative examples supporting the shift in the model's internal reasoning process.
    *   Focus on Korean, providing a specific and valuable example of how to address language bias.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The specific Korean reasoning dataset used for SFT may limit broader applicability.
    *   The computational cost of incorporating an oracle judge in the RL loop is likely high.
    *   The improvement on general knowledge benchmarks (MMLU) is minimal.
    *   The reliance on external APIs or a very large "judge" model at runtime raises concerns around deployment costs and latency, but the paper does propose future work into mitigating this with a smaller distilled judge model.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the individual components are not groundbreaking theoretical innovations, the careful combination and engineering of SFT and RL, the specific solution to stabilizing GRPO, and the demonstrable results in shifting the internal reasoning process make this a significant applied contribution. The emphasis on addressing a real-world problem (language bias) and the potential impact on inclusivity further increase the value.  The paper provides a valuable recipe and insights for adapting LLMs to other languages.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### What to Ask Next? Probing the Imaginative Reasoning of LLMs with TurtleSoup Puzzles
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10358v1)
- **Authors**: Mengtao Zhou, Sifan Wu, Huan Zhang, Qi Sima, Bang Liu
- **Abstract**: We investigate the capacity of Large Language Models (LLMs) for imaginative reasoning--the proactive construction, testing, and revision of hypotheses in information-sparse environments. Existing benchmarks, often static or focused on social deduction, fail to capture the dynamic, exploratory nature of this reasoning process. To address this gap, we introduce a comprehensive research framework based on the classic "Turtle Soup" game, integrating a benchmark, an agent, and an evaluation protocol. We present TurtleSoup-Bench, the first large-scale, bilingual, interactive benchmark for imaginative reasoning, comprising 800 turtle soup puzzles sourced from both the Internet and expert authors. We also propose Mosaic-Agent, a novel agent designed to assess LLMs' performance in this setting. To evaluate reasoning quality, we develop a multi-dimensional protocol measuring logical consistency, detail completion, and conclusion alignment. Experiments with leading LLMs reveal clear capability limits, common failure patterns, and a significant performance gap compared to humans. Our work offers new insights into LLMs' imaginative reasoning and establishes a foundation for future research on exploratory agent behavior.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "What to Ask Next? Probing the Imaginative Reasoning of LLMs with TurtleSoup Puzzles" introduces a new benchmark, *TurtleSoup-Bench*, designed to assess the imaginative reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). It's based on the classic "Turtle Soup" game, where solvers uncover a hidden story by asking yes/no questions about a terse initial scenario.  The paper also presents *Mosaic-Agent*, a multi-agent framework (Questioner, Responder, Memory) designed to solve these puzzles automatically.  The authors develop a multi-dimensional evaluation protocol measuring logical consistency, detail completion, and conclusion alignment. Experiments with leading LLMs reveal capability limitations and failure patterns compared to human performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in a few areas. First, its use of Turtle Soup puzzles as a *dynamic* and *interactive* environment for probing imaginative reasoning. Unlike existing benchmarks which are often static, *TurtleSoup-Bench* requires LLMs to actively explore a problem space, forming and revising hypotheses. Second, the proposed *Mosaic-Agent* framework provides a specific architecture designed to tackle these interactive puzzle-solving scenarios, explicitly modelling deliberation, meta-cognition, and action generation. Finally, the multi-dimensional evaluation metric moves beyond simple outcome-based scores to assess the *process* of reasoning, which is valuable.

*   **Significance:** The significance is two-fold. From an evaluation perspective, *TurtleSoup-Bench* exposes clear limitations in LLMs' imaginative reasoning abilities, even in state-of-the-art models. The error analysis provides insights into common failure modes like semantic fixation, context construction failures, logic blind spots, and deductive pruning failures. This information is useful for guiding future research directions. Secondly, The paper contributes to a more nuanced understanding of LLM capabilities beyond simple knowledge retrieval or social deduction by focusing on information scarce reasoning capabilities.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The introduction of *TurtleSoup-Bench* as a benchmark that fills a void in measuring interactive reasoning.
    *   A multi-agent framework *Mosaic-Agent* that provides architectural insights on how to build an agent for this task.
    *   The multi-dimensional evaluation protocol that captures the reasoning process in addition to the final result.
    *   A thorough error analysis that pinpoints specific weaknesses of LLMs in this domain.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experiments may not have investigated the full range of potential improvements or adaptations that could enhance LLM performance on the task.
    *   While Mosaic-Agent is a valuable contribution, it might be relatively specific to TurtleSoup puzzles, limiting its generalizability to other information-scarce reasoning tasks.
    *   While the error analysis is well-conducted, further investigating the root causes of the weaknesses would deepen our knowledge about LLMs. For instance, what modifications would be required in pre-training data, agent's architecture or fine-tuning approach to address these issues.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper could influence the design of new LLM architectures and training strategies focused on improving imaginative reasoning and dealing with incomplete information.
    *   The benchmark and evaluation protocol could be adopted by other researchers in the field, leading to a more standardized way of assessing these capabilities.
    *   The error analysis could inform targeted interventions to address specific weaknesses in LLMs, leading to more robust and capable agents.

**Overall Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a genuinely novel benchmark and framework for evaluating a previously under-explored dimension of LLM reasoning. *TurtleSoup-Bench* addresses a gap in the existing landscape of LLM evaluation by targeting interactive, information-scarce reasoning, an area where LLMs are notably weak. *Mosaic-Agent* provides a tangible architectural framework for agents attempting such problems. While the *Mosaic-Agent* design might have limitations in generalizability, and the experimental scope could be extended, the introduction of *TurtleSoup-Bench* and the associated insights derived from the error analysis make this a valuable and significant contribution to the field of LLM research.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Advancing Cross-lingual Aspect-Based Sentiment Analysis with LLMs and Constrained Decoding for Sequence-to-Sequence Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10366v1)
- **Authors**: Jakub Šmíd, Pavel Přibáň, Pavel Král
- **Abstract**: Aspect-based sentiment analysis (ABSA) has made significant strides, yet challenges remain for low-resource languages due to the predominant focus on English. Current cross-lingual ABSA studies often centre on simpler tasks and rely heavily on external translation tools. In this paper, we present a novel sequence-to-sequence method for compound ABSA tasks that eliminates the need for such tools. Our approach, which uses constrained decoding, improves cross-lingual ABSA performance by up to 10\%. This method broadens the scope of cross-lingual ABSA, enabling it to handle more complex tasks and providing a practical, efficient alternative to translation-dependent techniques. Furthermore, we compare our approach with large language models (LLMs) and show that while fine-tuned multilingual LLMs can achieve comparable results, English-centric LLMs struggle with these tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of cross-lingual aspect-based sentiment analysis (ABSA), specifically for compound tasks like end-to-end ABSA (E2E-ABSA), aspect category term extraction (ACTE), and target aspect category detection (TASD). The authors introduce a novel sequence-to-sequence approach that *doesn't* rely on external translation tools, a common practice in cross-lingual ABSA.  The key innovation is using constrained decoding during the sequence generation process to ensure the model predicts aspect terms in the target language, thus improving zero-shot cross-lingual transfer performance. They compare their method with large language models (LLMs) like GPT-4o and fine-tuned LLaMA models, showing that while fine-tuned multilingual LLMs can achieve comparable results, English-centric LLMs struggle.  They achieve new state-of-the-art results on benchmark datasets in five languages for both cross-lingual and monolingual ABSA. The experiments include E2E-ABSA, ACTE, and TASD.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel method. The combination of sequence-to-sequence models *with* constrained decoding for *complex* cross-lingual ABSA tasks is a significant departure from previous work, which often focused on simpler tasks or relied heavily on external translation. The study of LLMs for these specific complex ABSA tasks and their cross-lingual capability is also a worthwhile contribution. The novelty score will be adjusted downwards slightly because constrained decoding isn't entirely a novel concept, but its application in *this specific way* for cross-lingual ABSA is.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in several areas:
    *   **Practicality:**  By eliminating the reliance on external translation tools, the proposed method offers a more practical and potentially more efficient approach to cross-lingual ABSA. Translation introduces additional points of failure.

    *   **Improved Performance:** The constrained decoding strategy demonstrably improves zero-shot cross-lingual performance, allowing for better transfer of knowledge from a source language (English) to other languages.  The reported performance gains over existing methods and LLMs are substantial.

    *   **Broader Scope:** The paper extends the scope of cross-lingual ABSA to handle more complex tasks, making it relevant to a wider range of real-world applications. Addressing the more complex TASD/ACTE tasks is valuable.

    *   **LLM Evaluation:** The comparative analysis with LLMs, particularly GPT-4o and LLaMA, provides valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of these models for cross-lingual ABSA. Showing *how* and *why* they fail where the presented method succeeds is crucial for the community.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper includes a thorough experimental evaluation across multiple tasks, languages, and models.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The inclusion of an ablation study (comparing with and without constrained decoding) clearly demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed technique.
    *   **Error Analysis:** The error analysis provides valuable insights into the limitations of the models and identifies areas for future improvement.
    *   **Clear Writing:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited LLM Finetuning Depth:** The depth of fine-tuning used for LLMs could be explored more extensively. While QLoRA and LoRA are efficient, more comprehensive fine-tuning strategies might yield different results, though this would significantly increase computational costs. The relatively short fine-tuning duration might have unfairly disadvantaged the LLMs.
    *   **Data Scaling:** Experiments on even larger datasets could be performed to see if the results hold on data-rich scenarios. While zero-shot performance is a focus, real-world deployment often involves some data in the target language.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in cross-lingual ABSA by promoting the use of sequence-to-sequence models with constrained decoding. The insights gained from the LLM comparison are also valuable for guiding future research directions. It challenges the trend to solely rely on LLMs for all NLP tasks.
* **Justification:** the paper proposes a fairly novel method and achieves excellent results on difficult cross-lingual settings.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper provides a valuable and novel approach to cross-lingual ABSA. The results are strong, the analysis is thorough, and the potential influence is significant. However, the limited exploration of LLM fine-tuning and the potential for further scalability could be areas for future improvement, thus preventing a higher score. It makes a clear, demonstrable, and innovative advancement beyond the state of the art for compound cross-lingual ABSA.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10
