### When Language Overrules: Revealing Text Dominance in Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10552v1)
- **Authors**: Huyu Wu, Meng Tang, Xinhan Zheng, Haiyun Jiang
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across a diverse range of multimodal tasks. However, these models suffer from a core problem known as text dominance: they depend heavily on text for their inference, while underutilizing other modalities. While prior work has acknowledged this phenomenon in vision-language tasks, often attributing it to data biases or model architectures. In this paper, we conduct the first systematic investigation of text dominance across diverse data modalities, including images, videos, audio, time-series, and graphs. To measure this imbalance, we propose two evaluation metrics: the Modality Dominance Index (MDI) and the Attention Efficiency Index (AEI). Our comprehensive analysis reveals that text dominance is both significant and pervasive across all tested modalities. Our in-depth analysis identifies three underlying causes: attention dilution from severe token redundancy in non-textual modalities, the influence of fusion architecture design, and task formulations that implicitly favor textual inputs. Furthermore, we propose a simple token compression method that effectively rebalances model attention. Applying this method to LLaVA-7B, for instance, drastically reduces its MDI from 10.23 to a well-balanced value of 0.86. Our analysis and methodological framework offer a foundation for the development of more equitable and comprehensive multimodal language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the phenomenon of "text dominance" in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), where the model relies heavily on textual inputs while underutilizing other modalities like images, videos, audio, time-series data, and graphs.  The authors conduct a systematic analysis across various modalities and models, proposing two metrics, the Modality Dominance Index (MDI) and Attention Efficiency Index (AEI), to quantify this imbalance. They identify three main causes: token redundancy in non-text modalities, fusion architecture design, and task formulations that favor textual inputs. The paper proposes a simple token compression method to rebalance model attention and demonstrates its effectiveness on LLaVA-7B, significantly reducing its MDI.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in several aspects:

*   **Systematic Cross-Modal Analysis:** It goes beyond the common image-text pairing to analyze text dominance across a broader range of modalities, including video, audio, time-series, and graphs. This is a significant contribution because it reveals the pervasiveness of the issue beyond the vision-language domain. Prior work largely focused on image-text datasets.

*   **Attention-Based Metrics:** The proposal of MDI and AEI provides a quantitative way to measure and compare modality dominance and attention efficiency.  This offers a more rigorous and interpretable approach than simply observing the model's output.

*   **Root Cause Analysis:**  The identified causes – token redundancy, fusion architecture, and task formulation – are insightful. While data bias was a known issue, explicitly connecting these factors to the *attention mechanism itself* and demonstrating their impact across modalities is a novel contribution. Prior works typically treated the biases as extrinsic, while this paper highlights how these factors specifically dilute attention allocation *within* the model.

*   **Token Compression Solution:** While token compression isn't entirely new, its application in *specifically addressing modality imbalance by strategically reducing redundant non-text tokens* is a novel application and a practical solution that's easy to implement.

**Significance:**

The paper has significant implications for the development of more balanced and effective MLLMs:

*   **Improved Understanding:**  The analysis provides a deeper understanding of how MLLMs process information from different modalities and the biases that can arise.
*   **Targeted Mitigation Strategies:**  The identified causes and the proposed token compression method offer a clear path toward developing targeted mitigation strategies to address text dominance.
*   **Guidance for Model Design:** The paper's insights into the impact of fusion architecture design can guide the development of more equitable multimodal architectures.
*   **Framework for Evaluation:**  The MDI and AEI metrics provide a standardized framework for evaluating modality balance in MLLMs, which can facilitate future research in this area.

**Strengths:**

*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper employs a rigorous methodology, including controlled experiments and quantitative analysis.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents the research findings.
*   **Practical Solution:** The proposed token compression method is simple, effective, and easy to implement.
*   **Broad Applicability:** The findings are relevant to a wide range of MLLMs and multimodal tasks.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Solution:** The token compression method addresses only *one* of the identified causes (token redundancy).  While effective in its domain, it doesn't fully address the impact of fusion architecture or task formulation.
*   **Simplicity of Token Compression:** While easy to implement, the token compression method is relatively simple and may not be optimal for all modalities or tasks. The study relies on the CLS token, a design consideration that limits generalizability.  More advanced token selection techniques could be explored.
*   **Generalizability of Results:** The experiments primarily focus on a few specific models (LLaVA-1.5-7B, Qwen2.5-VL, etc.) and benchmarks.  While representative, it's important to validate the findings on a broader range of models and tasks.
*   **Lack of Qualitative Analysis:**  The paper is primarily quantitative.  Including qualitative examples of how token compression affects the model's reasoning and output would further strengthen the analysis.  Seeing *how* the model's output changes when it attends more to visual tokens would offer a richer understanding.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of MLLMs by systematically investigating text dominance across diverse modalities, providing insightful explanations for its occurrence, and offering a practical solution. The proposal of MDI and AEI, along with the root cause analysis, represents a significant advancement in our understanding of modality imbalance in MLLMs.  While the proposed solution is relatively simple and has limitations, it serves as a strong proof-of-concept and a starting point for further research. The findings are likely to influence future research in this area and guide the development of more balanced multimodal models.

However, the limitations discussed above, particularly the limited scope of the solution and the need for more comprehensive validation, prevent the paper from achieving a higher score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### eDIF: A European Deep Inference Fabric for Remote Interpretability of LLM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10553v1)
- **Authors**: Irma Heithoff. Marc Guggenberger, Sandra Kalogiannis, Susanne Mayer, Fabian Maag, Sigurd Schacht, Carsten Lanquillon
- **Abstract**: This paper presents a feasibility study on the deployment of a European Deep Inference Fabric (eDIF), an NDIF-compatible infrastructure designed to support mechanistic interpretability research on large language models. The need for widespread accessibility of LLM interpretability infrastructure in Europe drives this initiative to democratize advanced model analysis capabilities for the research community. The project introduces a GPU-based cluster hosted at Ansbach University of Applied Sciences and interconnected with partner institutions, enabling remote model inspection via the NNsight API. A structured pilot study involving 16 researchers from across Europe evaluated the platform's technical performance, usability, and scientific utility. Users conducted interventions such as activation patching, causal tracing, and representation analysis on models including GPT-2 and DeepSeek-R1-70B. The study revealed a gradual increase in user engagement, stable platform performance throughout, and a positive reception of the remote experimentation capabilities. It also marked the starting point for building a user community around the platform. Identified limitations such as prolonged download durations for activation data as well as intermittent execution interruptions are addressed in the roadmap for future development. This initiative marks a significant step towards widespread accessibility of LLM interpretability infrastructure in Europe and lays the groundwork for broader deployment, expanded tooling, and sustained community collaboration in mechanistic interpretability research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a feasibility study for deploying a European Deep Inference Fabric (eDIF), which is designed to support mechanistic interpretability research on large language models (LLMs. The eDIF is intended to be compatible with the National Deep Inference Fabric (NDIF) and aims to provide European researchers with accessible infrastructure for in-depth model analysis, circumventing the limited access to advanced systems. A GPU-based cluster at Ansbach University was interconnected with partner institutions to enable remote model inspection via the NNsight API.  A pilot study involving 16 researchers from across Europe evaluated the platform's technical performance, usability, and scientific utility using models like GPT-2 and DeepSeek-R1-70B. The results indicated positive reception, stable performance, growing user engagement, and the identification of areas for improvement, such as reducing download times and handling execution interruptions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper doesn't introduce radically new *algorithms* or *theoretical frameworks* in mechanistic interpretability. Its primary novelty lies in the *implementation* and *evaluation* of a European-based infrastructure for democratizing access to existing tools (NNsight, NDIF) and models. The core ideas of NDIF itself have been established; eDIF is essentially a regional adaptation and evaluation. While valuable, this limits its fundamental novelty. The porting of the NDIF architecture to different HPC environments (FAU), though presenting engineering challenges, isn't conceptually groundbreaking.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a critical need: the unequal distribution of computational resources in AI research, especially in Europe. By providing a shared, remotely accessible platform, eDIF tackles the barrier of entry for many academic groups that lack dedicated HPC access. Furthermore, the systematic evaluation provides practical insights for building similar infrastructure initiatives, focusing on usability, reproducibility, and alignment with European research goals.  The reported user satisfaction and the identified areas for improvement are valuable for guiding the future development of the eDIF and similar projects.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical focus:** The paper clearly addresses a real-world problem (resource constraints in LLM research).
    *   **Systematic evaluation:** The feasibility study involved real users, provided quantitative metrics, and captured qualitative feedback on usability and performance.
    *   **Clear problem definition and solution:** The paper effectively articulates the need for accessible infrastructure and proposes a concrete solution.
    *   **Reproducibility focus:**  Alignment with the NDIF framework and use of open tools enhance reproducibility.
    *   **Emphasis on democratization:** The focus on lowering barriers to entry for researchers is commendable.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited algorithmic innovation:** The paper doesn't introduce new interpretability methods; it focuses on enabling the *application* of existing ones.
    *   **Dependency on existing infrastructure (NDIF):** While beneficial for compatibility, it restricts independent innovation in the infrastructure itself.
    *   **Limited HPC porting details:** The challenges and solutions involved in porting to the AMD-based HPC environment could be described in greater depth. The full-scale deployment was also not pursued due to time restraints.
    *   **Feedback bias:** Low feedback participation by the users could limit the comprehensiveness of insight analysis.
    *   **Limited scalability analysis:** The scalability analysis, though mentioned, isn't presented with significant quantitative detail in the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** The eDIF can serve as a blueprint for similar regional or national infrastructure initiatives. The lessons learned from the feasibility study regarding onboarding, performance optimization, and user support will be highly valuable for other projects aiming to democratize access to AI research resources.

**Rationale:**

The paper is well-written, addresses a relevant problem, and provides practical value. However, its lack of deep algorithmic or theoretical novelty prevents it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution.  While significant from an engineering and resource democratization perspective, it builds heavily on existing work. The paper's strongest contribution lies in its thorough evaluation and the practical insights gleaned from it, serving as a strong reference for others seeking to establish similar infrastructure.
